### MaLAware: Automating the Comprehension of Malicious Software Behaviours using Large Language Models (LLMs)
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01145v1)
- **Authors**: Bikash Saha, Nanda Rani, Sandeep Kumar Shukla
- **Abstract**: Current malware (malicious software) analysis tools focus on detection and family classification but fail to provide clear and actionable narrative insights into the malignant activity of the malware. Therefore, there is a need for a tool that translates raw malware data into human-readable descriptions. Developing such a tool accelerates incident response, reduces malware analysts' cognitive load, and enables individuals having limited technical expertise to understand malicious software behaviour. With this objective, we present MaLAware, which automatically summarizes the full spectrum of malicious activity of malware executables. MaLAware processes Cuckoo Sandbox-generated reports using large language models (LLMs) to correlate malignant activities and generate concise summaries explaining malware behaviour. We evaluate the tool's performance on five open-source LLMs. The evaluation uses the human-written malware behaviour description dataset as ground truth. The model's performance is measured using 11 extensive performance metrics, which boosts the confidence of MaLAware's effectiveness. The current version of the tool, i.e., MaLAware, supports Qwen2.5-7B, Llama2-7B, Llama3.1-8B, Mistral-7B, and Falcon-7B, along with the quantization feature for resource-constrained environments. MaLAware lays a foundation for future research in malware behavior explanation, and its extensive evaluation demonstrates LLMs' ability to narrate malware behavior in an actionable and comprehensive manner.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MaLAware: Automating the Comprehension of Malicious Software Behaviors using Large Language Models (LLMs)":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MaLAware, a tool that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to automate the comprehension and summarization of malicious software behaviors. MaLAware processes Cuckoo Sandbox reports, filters irrelevant information, and feeds the processed data into an LLM to generate human-readable summaries of malware activities. The paper evaluates the performance of five open-source LLMs (Qwen2.5-7B, Llama2-7B, Llama3.1-8B, Mistral-7B, and Falcon-7B) using a manually created dataset of 133 malware samples and a set of 11 evaluation metrics. The results show that Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct performs best, demonstrating superior semantic and contextual accuracy. The tool also includes a quantization feature for resource-constrained environments. The authors emphasize the potential of MaLAware to improve incident response, threat intelligence, and communication of security threats.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs to summarize malware analysis reports is somewhat novel, but not groundbreaking.  Existing research has explored using AI and NLP for various aspects of malware analysis, including detection, family classification, and even reverse engineering. However, focusing on generating actionable, human-readable summaries directly from sandbox reports is a valuable contribution that addresses a practical need. The approach of filtering Cuckoo sandbox reports and using 4-bit quantization is a good technique for a constrained computational environment.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to bridge the gap between technical malware analysis data and actionable insights for a broader audience, including security analysts with varying levels of expertise, executives, and legal teams. By automating the generation of clear summaries, MaLAware could reduce cognitive load, accelerate incident response, and improve overall cybersecurity posture. The release of the tool and dataset to the research community is also a significant contribution. The use of 11 diverse evaluation metrics boosts the confidence of MaLAware's effectiveness.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Application:** The paper addresses a real-world problem in cybersecurity and offers a practical solution.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The evaluation using multiple LLMs and a diverse set of metrics provides a solid assessment of the tool's performance.
    *   **Resource Efficiency:**  The quantization feature makes the tool accessible even in resource-constrained environments.
    *   **Open-Source Contribution:** The authors make the tool and dataset publicly available, promoting further research and development.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset:** The dataset of 133 malware samples is relatively small, which may limit the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Open-Source Model Reliance:** Restricting the evaluation to open-source LLMs limits a full comparative view of what black-box models like GPT-4 could achieve.
    *   **Lack of Fine-Tuning:**  The LLMs were not fine-tuned for the specific task of malware behavior explanation, which could potentially improve their performance. The paper also mentions that this limitation is to be addressed in future work.
    *   **Limited Evaluation Data:**  The dataset for creating and evaluating LLM output is rather small given the breadth of capabilities modern LLMs have, therefore a comprehensive evaluation has to be taken with a grain of salt.

*   **Potential Influence:** MaLAware has the potential to influence the field by providing a valuable tool for automating malware analysis and improving communication of security threats. It could also inspire further research on using LLMs for other cybersecurity tasks. The extensive evaluation demonstrates the LLM's ability to narrate malware behaviour in an actionable and comprehensive manner.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a worthwhile application of LLMs to a practical problem in cybersecurity. While the core idea is not entirely novel, the specific focus on generating actionable summaries from sandbox reports and the comprehensive evaluation contribute to its significance. However, the limitations in dataset size and the lack of LLM fine-tuning hold back the potential impact. Despite these limitations, the tool's resource efficiency and open-source availability further increases its potential impact.

### Catch Me if You Search: When Contextual Web Search Results Affect the Detection of Hallucinations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01153v1)
- **Authors**: Mahjabin Nahar, Eun-Ju Lee, Jin Won Park, Dongwon Lee
- **Abstract**: While we increasingly rely on large language models (LLMs) for various tasks, these models are known to produce inaccurate content or 'hallucinations' with potentially disastrous consequences. The recent integration of web search results into LLMs prompts the question of whether people utilize them to verify the generated content, thereby avoiding falling victim to hallucinations. This study (N = 560) investigated how the provision of search results, either static (fixed search results) or dynamic (participant-driven searches), affect participants' perceived accuracy and confidence in evaluating LLM-generated content (i.e., genuine, minor hallucination, major hallucination), compared to the control condition (no search results). Findings indicate that participants in both static and dynamic conditions (vs. control) rated hallucinated content to be less accurate. However, those in the dynamic condition rated genuine content as more accurate and demonstrated greater overall confidence in their assessments than those in the static or control conditions. In addition, those higher in need for cognition (NFC) rated major hallucinations to be less accurate than low NFC participants, with no corresponding difference for genuine content or minor hallucinations. These results underscore the potential benefits of integrating web search results into LLMs for the detection of hallucinations, as well as the need for a more nuanced approach when developing human-centered systems, taking user characteristics into account.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how the presence of web search results affects people's ability to detect hallucinations in large language model (LLM) generated content.  The study compares three conditions: a control group with no search results, a "static" group where pre-retrieved search results are provided, and a "dynamic" group where participants can conduct their own searches.  The authors found that participants in both search conditions were better at identifying hallucinations than those in the control condition.  Interestingly, those in the dynamic search condition rated genuine content as more accurate and were more confident in their assessments. They also found that individuals high in need for cognition (NFC) were better at detecting major hallucinations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Relevant Problem:** The paper addresses a highly relevant and timely problem: the detection of hallucinations in LLM outputs. As LLMs become more integrated into various applications, understanding how humans can effectively identify inaccuracies is crucial.
    *   **Well-Designed Experiment:** The study uses a relatively large sample size (N=560) and a well-controlled experimental design with three conditions and careful stimulus generation using multiple LLMs to create genuine and hallucinated responses. The use of a Latin square design further strengthens the rigor of the study.
    *   **Interesting Findings:** The results offer valuable insights. The finding that both static and dynamic search results improve hallucination detection compared to no search results is practically significant. The added benefit of dynamic search for assessing genuine content suggests a potential path for building more effective human-AI collaboration. Also, the role of NFC offers nuances on how people evaluate LLM-generated content.
    *   **Analysis of Search Results:** The analysis of search result quality is a plus. It attempts to rule out the possibility that the improved detection in the search conditions was simply due to better search results.
    * **Clear and Well Organized:** The writing is clear and well-organized, making the paper easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The use of Prolific workers from the US limits the generalizability of the findings to other populations.  The study acknowledges this limitation.
    *   **Simplified Search Task:** Pre-populating search queries in the dynamic condition, while intended to promote consistency, might have constrained the search process and affected the results. A more open-ended search task could reveal different patterns.
    *   **Lack of Depth in User Behavior Analysis:** The study does not delve deeply into how participants used the search results. Eye-tracking or think-aloud protocols could provide valuable insights into their information-seeking strategies and decision-making processes. The data collection for the dynamic condition only collected the screenshot of the final search result, limiting insights into how users iteratively refine their search process.
    *   **Search Result Quality:** While the authors analyzed search result quality, it's difficult to fully control for the variability in search results that participants might encounter, even within a short time window.
    *   **Limited theoretical grounding**: While the paper mentions HSM theory, the theoretical connection to the current findings is not well-established. A more thorough discussion on how different theories can explain the results could strengthen the contribution of the paper.
    * **Limited Novelty**: The combination of RAG and human-LLM interaction has been explored in the literature. This paper's novelty comes from directly comparing static vs dynamic search within a user-centric LLM evaluation context, and further examining the impact on the different levels of hallucinations.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper has practical implications for the design of LLM-based systems.  The findings suggest that integrating search results into LLMs can improve human accuracy in evaluating generated content. The findings also provide useful nuances regarding the impact of the dynamic search.
    *   It contributes to the growing body of research on human-AI collaboration and the challenges of detecting misinformation generated by LLMs.
    *   The inclusion of NFC as a factor is a valuable addition, highlighting the importance of considering individual differences in cognitive processing.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper addresses a significant problem with a well-designed experiment and provides valuable insights into the effect of web search results on hallucination detection. It has practical implications for the design of LLM-based systems. However, the limitations regarding generalizability, the somewhat constrained search task, and the lack of in-depth user behavior analysis temper its overall impact. While the paper is not groundbreaking, it provides incremental contributions and raises important questions.

### Beyond Quacking: Deep Integration of Language Models and RAG into DuckDB
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01157v1)
- **Authors**: Anas Dorbani, Sunny Yasser, Jimmy Lin, Amine Mhedhbi
- **Abstract**: Knowledge-intensive analytical applications retrieve context from both structured tabular data and unstructured, text-free documents for effective decision-making. Large language models (LLMs) have made it significantly easier to prototype such retrieval and reasoning data pipelines. However, implementing these pipelines efficiently still demands significant effort and has several challenges. This often involves orchestrating heterogeneous data systems, managing data movement, and handling low-level implementation details, e.g., LLM context management. To address these challenges, we introduce FlockMTL: an extension for DBMSs that deeply integrates LLM capabilities and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). FlockMTL includes model-driven scalar and aggregate functions, enabling chained predictions through tuple-level mappings and reductions. Drawing inspiration from the relational model, FlockMTL incorporates: (i) cost-based optimizations, which seamlessly apply techniques such as batching and caching; and (ii) resource independence, enabled through novel SQL DDL abstractions: PROMPT and MODEL, introduced as first-class schema objects alongside TABLE. FlockMTL streamlines the development of knowledge-intensive analytical applications, and its optimizations ease the implementation burden.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Beyond Quacking: Deep Integration of Language Models and RAG into DuckDB":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FLOCKMTL, a DuckDB extension that deeply integrates Large Language Model (LLM) capabilities and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) into a database management system (DBMS).  FLOCKMTL provides model-driven scalar and aggregate functions that enable chained predictions through tuple-level mappings and reductions.  Inspired by the relational model, it incorporates cost-based optimizations (batching, caching) and resource independence (through new SQL DDL abstractions PROMPT and MODEL). The paper emphasizes how FLOCKMTL simplifies the development of knowledge-intensive analytical applications by abstracting away low-level implementation details.  The paper also demonstrates FLOCKMTL's utility through examples of semantic filtering, summarization, keyword extraction, and hybrid search pipelines. It also introduces a demonstration application with a natural language interface and plan debugging to make its usage more accessible.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical Integration:** The paper addresses a real-world challenge: the complexity of building LLM-powered applications that require tight integration with structured data. FLOCKMTL offers a pragmatic solution by integrating LLMs directly into a DBMS, rather than relying on external orchestration. This simplifies data pipelines and reduces data shuffling.
    *   **Cost-based Optimization:** Incorporating cost-based optimization, batching, and caching, are smart decisions as they tackle key performance bottlenecks in LLM-integrated workflows. This makes the framework practical for real-world deployment.
    *   **Resource Abstraction:** The MODEL and PROMPT DDL abstractions provide a clean way to manage and update LLM models and prompts without modifying application logic. This enhances maintainability and reusability.
    *   **Flexibility and Extensibility:** FLOCKMTL provides a comprehensive range of functions, from scalar to aggregate, enabling users to develop complex analytical pipelines, allowing for hybrid search.
    *   **Demonstration and Accessibility:** The paper features a demonstration app with plan inspection, helping users understand and optimize FLOCKMTL queries. This promotes adoption and reduces the learning curve.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Experimental Evaluation:** While the paper mentions performance gains from batching, it lacks a comprehensive experimental evaluation comparing FLOCKMTL against alternative approaches. More quantitative results would strengthen the paper's claims. The evaluation appears to be limited to user interaction.
    *   **Scalability Concerns:** The paper does not thoroughly address the scalability challenges of running LLMs directly within a DBMS, especially for large datasets and complex queries. How FLOCKMTL handles concurrency and resource management at scale is unclear.
    *   **Reliance on DuckDB:** The extension is tied to DuckDB, and the degree of portability to other DBMSs isn't discussed. This limits the applicability of FLOCKMTL.
    *   **Specific Models/Providers Mentioned:** While OpenAI and Azure are mentioned, the generality of the approach across LLM providers (e.g., support for local models) could be clearer.  There is a brief mention of local Ollama support, but it isn't clearly emphasized.
    *   **Novelty within SQL Community:** The actual functions themselves, while useful, may not be revolutionary in the broader context of SQL extensions. The true novelty is the integration of a very hot topic in a way that abstracts away considerable complexity from the user.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper's significance lies in its practical approach to integrating LLMs into a DBMS for knowledge-intensive analytics. It simplifies the development process, provides key optimizations, and offers a clean abstraction for managing LLM resources. FLOCKMTL has the potential to significantly improve the efficiency and accessibility of LLM-powered data applications.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of data management by providing a practical and well-engineered solution for integrating LLMs into a DBMS.  The focus on usability and optimization makes FLOCKMTL a promising tool for building real-world applications.  However, the lack of extensive experimental evaluation and the dependence on DuckDB limit its current impact. The paper also has a slightly "demo-ware" feel, and the novelty is somewhat incremental.

### Predicting Field Experiments with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01167v1)
- **Authors**: Yaoyu Chen, Yuheng Hu, Yingda Lu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated unprecedented emergent capabilities, including content generation, translation, and the simulation of human behavior. Field experiments, despite their high cost, are widely employed in economics and the social sciences to study real-world human behavior through carefully designed manipulations and treatments. However, whether and how these models can be utilized to predict outcomes of field experiments remains unclear. In this paper, we propose and evaluate an automated LLM-based framework that produces predictions of field experiment outcomes. Applying this framework to 319 experiments drawn from renowned economics literature yields a notable prediction accuracy of 78%. Interestingly, we find that performance is highly skewed. We attribute this skewness to several factors, including gender differences, ethnicity, and social norms.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper "Predicting Field Experiments with Large Language Models" explores the ability of LLMs to predict the outcomes of real-world field experiments, a notoriously complex area due to participant diversity and multifaceted designs. The authors propose an automated framework leveraging GPT, which includes information extraction, variant generation, and prediction modules. This framework is tested on over 300 economics field experiments with 1638 conclusions extracted from top-tier journals. The results show a 78% average prediction accuracy without fine-tuning. Notably, the prediction accuracy is highly skewed, with excellent performance on some experiments and complete failure on others. The paper further investigates this skewness, linking underperformance to experiments dealing with gender differences, ethnicity, and social norms, highlighting potential biases in LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has moderate novelty. The use of LLMs to *simulate* human behavior has been explored previously in the context of lab experiments. However, this paper is the first to tackle the much more challenging task of predicting outcomes in *field experiments*, which have greater complexity and real-world variability. The shift to automating the entire process at scale is a significant step, pushing beyond manual case studies.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant for several reasons:

    *   It demonstrates the *potential* for LLMs to analyze and potentially predict complex social phenomena studied via field experiments. This could open doors to using LLMs for pre-testing interventions, optimizing experimental design, or generating hypotheses.
    *   The identification of *biases* related to gender, ethnicity, and social norms is crucial. This highlights the limitations of LLMs in certain contexts and emphasizes the need for careful consideration of fairness and bias when using these models for social science research. This alone would be a very high-impact finding.
    *   The development of an *automated framework* is a valuable contribution. It enables the large-scale analysis of experiments and facilitates further research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper provides a well-defined methodology, outlining each stage of the framework clearly.
    *   **Large-Scale Evaluation:** Testing the framework on a sizable dataset of over 300 experiments strengthens the validity of the findings.
    *   **Focus on Limitations:** The identification and analysis of prediction limitations (related to bias) is a key strength and adds depth to the research.
    *   **Comparison and Contrast with Existing Methods**: The literature review does a good job of setting up what has been done before and how this work adds to the field.
    *   **Handling of Data Leakage**: Very specific choice of LLMs that were explicitly designed to prevent the potential for data leakage.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Economics-centric:** The focus solely on economics experiments limits the generalizability of the findings to other social science disciplines.
    *   **Reliance on Claude for Extraction:** While using Claude is justified to prevent data leakage, it also introduces the possibility of errors or limitations from that particular LLM. A more thorough error analysis of the extraction stage would be beneficial.
    *   **Fine-tuning Omitted:** The authors justified not doing any fine-tuning of the model to improve results and prevent data leakage. But without fine-tuning, the overall application of this model remains theoretical, as it seems to not be able to fully realize the potential of the model, and the limitations of the model.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to stimulate further research on using LLMs for social science prediction and simulation. The identified biases will also encourage work on mitigating these biases in LLMs.
    *   It provides a clear roadmap for creating an AI that could assist social scientists in their work.

Overall, the paper is a solid contribution that pushes the boundaries of using LLMs in social science. While not without its limitations, the paper demonstrates the promise of this approach and highlights the critical need to address biases.

**Score: 7.5**

*   **Rationale:** The paper has considerable novelty and is of significant interest to researchers in social science, economics, and AI. It has some limitations in generalizability and the reliance on a specific dataset. However, the insights gained from the experiments, especially related to biases, are valuable, and the automated framework opens opportunities for future research. With more refinement in the limitations section and inclusion of fine-tuning results, the paper could be ranked even higher.

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### Neural Approaches to SAT Solving: Design Choices and Interpretability
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01173v1)
- **Authors**: David Mojžíšek, Jan Hůla, Ziwei Li, Ziyu Zhou, Mikoláš Janota
- **Abstract**: In this contribution, we provide a comprehensive evaluation of graph neural networks applied to Boolean satisfiability problems, accompanied by an intuitive explanation of the mechanisms enabling the model to generalize to different instances. We introduce several training improvements, particularly a novel closest assignment supervision method that dynamically adapts to the model's current state, significantly enhancing performance on problems with larger solution spaces. Our experiments demonstrate the suitability of variable-clause graph representations with recurrent neural network updates, which achieve good accuracy on SAT assignment prediction while reducing computational demands. We extend the base graph neural network into a diffusion model that facilitates incremental sampling and can be effectively combined with classical techniques like unit propagation. Through analysis of embedding space patterns and optimization trajectories, we show how these networks implicitly perform a process very similar to continuous relaxations of MaxSAT, offering an interpretable view of their reasoning process. This understanding guides our design choices and explains the ability of recurrent architectures to scale effectively at inference time beyond their training distribution, which we demonstrate with test-time scaling experiments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Neural Approaches to SAT Solving: Design Choices and Interpretability":

**Summary:**

This paper presents a comprehensive evaluation of Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) for solving Boolean Satisfiability (SAT) problems. It explores different GNN architectures, training strategies, and graph representations (literal-clause graph (LCG) vs. variable-clause graph (VCG)). The authors introduce a novel closest assignment supervision method, which dynamically adapts to the model's current state and enhances performance, especially on problems with larger solution spaces. The paper extends a basic GNN into a diffusion model to facilitate incremental sampling, which can be combined with classical techniques like unit propagation. Further, the paper analyzes the trained models, showing that they implicitly perform a process similar to continuous relaxations of MaxSAT. The test-time scaling experiments reveal the ability of recurrent architectures to scale effectively at inference time beyond their training distribution.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough experimental comparison of various GNN architectures (RNN vs. LSTM), graph representations (LCG vs VCG), and training methods within the specific context of SAT solving.  This is a valuable contribution as it clarifies the trade-offs involved in each design choice.
    * **Novel Supervision Method:** The introduction of the "closest assignment" supervision method is a significant contribution.  The authors demonstrate its effectiveness in improving generalization, especially when dealing with SAT instances having numerous solutions.  The dynamic adaptation of the supervision signal is a well-motivated and potentially reusable technique.
    * **Interpretability:**  The analysis of the learned embeddings and the connection drawn to continuous relaxations of MaxSAT is a notable strength.  It provides a degree of interpretability to GNNs, which are often treated as black boxes. The claim that GNNs are performing something akin to a continuous optimization is interesting and warrants further investigation.
    * **Test-Time Scaling:** The demonstration of test-time scaling using the recurrent architecture is valuable. The ability to trade computation for performance at inference is a practical advantage that makes these models more adaptable. The demonstration of integrating diffusion with unit propagation is also interesting.
    * **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and structured, which aids in understanding the various design choices and experimental results.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Lack of comparison with current state-of-the-art SAT solvers:** The most significant weakness is the lack of direct comparison with state-of-the-art SAT solvers, especially CDCL solvers. The paper acknowledges this limitation but it dramatically reduces its practical impact. While the focus is on understanding neural reasoning, demonstrating even a small improvement over existing solvers would add significant value. The study fails to concretely demonstrate improvements over established techniques.
    * **Limited to Random Problems:** The paper relies exclusively on synthetically generated SAT instances (SR and 3-SAT). While this simplifies analysis and avoids superficial feature learning, it raises concerns about the generalizability of the findings to real-world SAT problems. The authors argue that models trained on these problems transfer well in other work by Li et al., but this external reference doesn't fully mitigate the concern.
    * **Computational Cost:** The closest assignment training method, while effective, is computationally expensive, particularly for large benchmarks. It is a significant bottleneck.
    * **The benefit of Diffusion Model is still unclear:** In the experiments the diffusion model still leverages multiple passes. In addition, it seems to require an assignment predictor which defeats the purpose of a diffusion model.
    * **Incremental nature of results:**  While each individual contribution is valuable, the paper largely builds upon existing work (NeuroSAT). This is fine but somewhat diminishes the novelty.

* **Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in: 1) the closest assignment training method, 2) the analysis of embedding space and connection to continuous relaxation methods, 3) the combination with diffusion models, and 4) the thorough comparison of different architectures and training objectives.

The significance is that it advances our understanding of how GNNs can learn to solve SAT problems and how to make them more effective and adaptable. It provides insights that can inform the design of more powerful neural solvers and hybrid algorithms.

**Overall Score:**

Score: 6

**Justification:**

The paper offers a valuable and detailed exploration of GNNs for SAT solving, contributes some original techniques (closest assignment, embedding analysis), and provides evidence of how GNNs may be implicitly learning to perform continuous optimization. These results are significant and relevant to the machine learning and neural reasoning communities. However, the lack of comparison with state-of-the-art SAT solvers, reliance on synthetic problems, and significant computational cost limitations hamper its practical significance and lower the overall score. While the findings are interesting and insightful, the limited real-world applicability and building off previous work prevent it from achieving a higher rating. The paper provides an **interesting but incremental advance** in our knowledge, warranting a score of 6.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### $μ$KE: Matryoshka Unstructured Knowledge Editing of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01196v1)
- **Authors**: Zian Su, Ziyang Huang, Kaiyuan Zhang, Xiangyu Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful knowledge bases yet are limited by static training data, leading to issues such as hallucinations and safety risks. Editing a model's internal knowledge through the locate-and-edit paradigm has proven a cost-effective alternative to retraining, though current unstructured approaches, especially window-based autoregressive methods, often disrupt the causal dependency between early memory updates and later output tokens. In this work, we first theoretically analyze these limitations and then introduce Matryoshka Unstructured Knowledge Editing ($\mu$KE), a novel memory update mechanism that preserves such dependencies via a Matryoshka-style objective and adaptive loss coefficients. Empirical evaluations on two models across four benchmarks demonstrate that $\mu$KE improves edit efficacy by up to 12.33% over state-of-the-art methods, and remain robust when applied to diverse formatted edits, underscoring its potential for effective unstructured knowledge editing in LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces µKE (Matryoshka Unstructured Knowledge Editing), a novel approach to unstructured knowledge editing in large language models (LLMs).  It addresses the limitations of existing window-based autoregressive methods, which often disrupt causal dependencies between early memory updates and later output tokens. µKE employs a Matryoshka-style objective and adaptive loss coefficients to preserve these dependencies. The approach decomposes the editing target into multiple 'target figures' (akin to windows) and uses a weighted sum of negative log-likelihood losses for all figures starting from a given memory update.  The adaptive coefficients, informed by affinity between edit target figures, balance the contribution of intermediate terms. Experimental results demonstrate improved edit efficacy and robustness across diverse formatted edits.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the µKE objective function and the use of adaptive loss coefficients. The analysis of limitations in window-based methods is insightful, and the Matryoshka-style objective directly addresses those limitations. The affinity-based adaptive coefficients offer a simple, gradient-informed method for dynamically balancing the optimization process, which is innovative in this context. The insight that dependency from former memories to later target outputs is not properly maintained by a purely autoregressive window update method is indeed novel.

*   **Significance:** Knowledge editing is a crucial area for maintaining and improving LLMs. The approach addresses a key shortcoming of existing unstructured editing methods, leading to tangible improvements in edit efficacy. The significance is further enhanced by demonstrating improvements in diverse formats. Moreover, the presented theoretical analysis and the µKE method for unstructured knowledge editing could have broad applications in other memory update scenarios, thereby increasing their significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Sound Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides clear theoretical motivations and analysis of the limitations of previous methods, offering a good justification for the proposed approach.

    *   **Well-Designed Methodology:** The µKE approach is well-defined and implementable. The Matryoshka-style objective and adaptive loss coefficients represent significant contributions to the field of unstructured editing.

    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments are comprehensive, using multiple models and benchmarks. The results demonstrate the superior performance of µKE compared to state-of-the-art methods, bolstering the approach's viability. The inclusion of metrics such as BLEU, Rouge-L, and BERTScore further strengthens the analysis.

    *   **Reproducible Research:** The paper shares a code repository which will encourage transparency and reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope for Batch Editing:** Although the adaptive loss coefficients attempt to address sample variability, the paper acknowledges that batch editing is a crucial aspect for practical application. The lack of a concrete solution in the current work is a notable limitation.

    *   **Dependency on Locate-and-Edit:** The approach is tied to the locate-and-edit paradigm. While this paradigm is popular, the µKE framework may not directly translate to other knowledge manipulation methods.

    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** Fine-tuning the window size and other hyperparameters can significantly affect model performance, and while the authors addressed the issue, the sensitivity might limit its practical use.

*   **Potential Influence:** This research is likely to influence subsequent work on knowledge editing, particularly in unstructured settings. The concepts of preserving causal dependencies and dynamically adjusting loss terms based on affinity can inform the design of future editing algorithms.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant improvement to unstructured knowledge editing. The theoretical grounding, well-designed methodology, and strong empirical results justify the claims. The weaknesses, while notable, are primarily related to scope and implementation practicalities rather than fundamental flaws. The potential influence on the field is significant, given the importance of knowledge editing. Taking all factors into consideration:

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Medical large language models are easily distracted
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01201v1)
- **Authors**: Krithik Vishwanath, Anton Alyakin, Daniel Alexander Alber, Jin Vivian Lee, Douglas Kondziolka, Eric Karl Oermann
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have the potential to transform medicine, but real-world clinical scenarios contain extraneous information that can hinder performance. The rise of assistive technologies like ambient dictation, which automatically generates draft notes from live patient encounters, has the potential to introduce additional noise making it crucial to assess the ability of LLM's to filter relevant data. To investigate this, we developed MedDistractQA, a benchmark using USMLE-style questions embedded with simulated real-world distractions. Our findings show that distracting statements (polysemous words with clinical meanings used in a non-clinical context or references to unrelated health conditions) can reduce LLM accuracy by up to 17.9%. Commonly proposed solutions to improve model performance such as retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and medical fine-tuning did not change this effect and in some cases introduced their own confounders and further degraded performance. Our findings suggest that LLMs natively lack the logical mechanisms necessary to distinguish relevant from irrelevant clinical information, posing challenges for real-world applications. MedDistractQA and our results highlights the need for robust mitigation strategies to enhance LLM resilience to extraneous information.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Medical large language models are easily distracted" introduces MedDistractQA, a benchmark designed to evaluate the robustness of large language models (LLMs) in the medical domain against extraneous information. This benchmark consists of USMLE-style questions with added distracting statements – either non-literal uses of medical terms or irrelevant medical details concerning third parties. The authors tested various LLMs, including proprietary and open-source models, and found that these distracting elements can significantly reduce accuracy. Common approaches like medical fine-tuning and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) did not consistently mitigate this effect, and in some cases, even worsened performance. The authors conclude that LLMs currently lack inherent logical mechanisms to differentiate relevant from irrelevant clinical information, posing a challenge for real-world medical applications and highlighting the need for more robust mitigation strategies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a crucial concern in the deployment of LLMs in medical settings: their susceptibility to noise and irrelevant information. While LLMs have shown promise in standardized medical exams, the reality of clinical practice involves navigating complex patient histories filled with potentially distracting details. The core strength of this work lies in its pragmatic approach to evaluating this vulnerability.

Here's a breakdown of strengths and weaknesses:

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Relevant Problem:** The problem of distraction in medical LLMs is highly relevant. Clinical settings are messy, and the ability to filter irrelevant information is crucial for reliable performance.
    *   **Well-Defined Benchmark:** MedDistractQA provides a quantitative and systematic way to assess LLM robustness. The methodology for creating distracting statements seems reasonable and well-explained.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study tests a range of models (proprietary and open-source) and explores the effects of common techniques like fine-tuning and RAG.
    *   **Counterintuitive Findings:** The observation that RAG can sometimes *degrade* performance due to the introduction of irrelevant context is significant and challenges conventional wisdom.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written, and the figures are informative.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Distractions:** The types of distractions considered – non-literal terms and third-party medical details – are somewhat limited. Real-world clinical notes may contain much more varied and complex forms of irrelevant information (e.g., irrelevant personal details, vague statements). More diverse distraction types would strengthen the benchmark.
    *   **Algorithmically Generated Distractions:** The distractions are generated algorithmically. While the process is explained, there's a possibility that these generated distractions are not entirely representative of the types of distractions present in real clinical data.
    *   **Reliance on Multiple-Choice Questions:** As acknowledged by the authors, the exclusive use of multiple-choice questions limits the generalizability of the findings. The ability to filter distractions might be different in open-ended question-answering or diagnostic scenarios.
    *   **Mechanistic Understanding:** While the paper demonstrates the vulnerability, it offers limited insight into *why* LLMs are susceptible to these distractions. A more in-depth analysis of the attention mechanisms or internal representations of the models could strengthen the paper.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The novelty primarily lies in the identification and systematic evaluation of a significant limitation of medical LLMs using a new and explicitly tailored benchmark. While previous research has explored the general susceptibility of LLMs to noise, this work is among the first to focus on the specific types of distractions that are likely to arise in medical contexts and to demonstrate how common solutions don't necessarily resolve them. The insight on the potential downsides of RAG is particularly valuable.

The significance resides in the call for more robust evaluation methodologies and for the development of techniques that genuinely improve LLM resilience to extraneous information in complex clinical environments. This has implications for future research directions in medical AI.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by highlighting a practical problem with deploying medical LLMs and providing a methodology for assessing this vulnerability. The counterintuitive findings regarding RAG contribute to a better understanding of these systems. However, the limitations related to the type of distractions, the algorithmic generation of distractions, and the multiple-choice question format prevent it from being a truly transformative work.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Articulated Kinematics Distillation from Video Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01204v1)
- **Authors**: Xuan Li, Qianli Ma, Tsung-Yi Lin, Yongxin Chen, Chenfanfu Jiang, Ming-Yu Liu, Donglai Xiang
- **Abstract**: We present Articulated Kinematics Distillation (AKD), a framework for generating high-fidelity character animations by merging the strengths of skeleton-based animation and modern generative models. AKD uses a skeleton-based representation for rigged 3D assets, drastically reducing the Degrees of Freedom (DoFs) by focusing on joint-level control, which allows for efficient, consistent motion synthesis. Through Score Distillation Sampling (SDS) with pre-trained video diffusion models, AKD distills complex, articulated motions while maintaining structural integrity, overcoming challenges faced by 4D neural deformation fields in preserving shape consistency. This approach is naturally compatible with physics-based simulation, ensuring physically plausible interactions. Experiments show that AKD achieves superior 3D consistency and motion quality compared with existing works on text-to-4D generation. Project page: https://research.nvidia.com/labs/dir/akd/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Articulated Kinematics Distillation (AKD), a framework designed to generate high-fidelity character animations by combining the strengths of skeleton-based animation and generative video diffusion models.  AKD uses a skeleton representation to significantly reduce the degrees of freedom (DoFs), allowing for efficient and consistent motion synthesis. It utilizes Score Distillation Sampling (SDS) with pre-trained video diffusion models to distill complex articulated motions while preserving structural integrity. The approach is compatible with physics-based simulation, ensuring physically plausible interactions. The authors demonstrate improved 3D consistency and motion quality compared to existing text-to-4D generation methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the integration of traditional skeleton-based rigging with modern video diffusion models.  Existing text-to-4D methods often struggle with 3D consistency due to the high dimensionality of deformation fields. By constraining motion through a skeleton, AKD offers a more structured and controllable approach. The use of non-uniform ground renderings to hint at physical plausibility is a nice touch.
* **Significance:** The significance is potentially high.  Character animation is a complex process, and simplifying it through generative models is a valuable goal.  AKD presents a promising step in that direction by addressing a key weakness of purely generative methods: the lack of explicit 3D structure. Compatibility with physics simulation also adds value.
* **Strengths:**
    *   **Improved 3D Consistency:**  The skeleton representation effectively regularizes the deformation space, leading to more structurally sound animations than methods relying solely on deformation fields.
    *   **Physics-Based Compatibility:**  The skeleton structure makes it straightforward to integrate physics-based simulation for added realism.
    *   **Efficient Distillation:** Reduction of DoFs leads to more efficient motion distillation.
    *   **Ground Rendering:** This adds a touch of realism and physicality.
* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Text-to-3D:** The framework depends on an external text-to-3D module for initial asset creation, which can limit the overall quality depending on the capability of that module. The paper indicates that assets from photogrammetry/artist are also compatible, but lack of direct integration would need additional work on rigging and skinning.
    *   **Limited Motion Diversity:**  The generated motion is constrained by the capabilities of the video diffusion model. Specific action may be difficult to achieve.
    *   **Manual Rigging:** The manual rigging process is a bottleneck to scalability. While automatic rigging tools exist, their integration isn't explicitly demonstrated in the paper. The performance on rigged characters is also a factor of the skeleton.

*   **Clarity and Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology. The figures and accompanying video are helpful in visualizing the results.

*   **Impact:** The proposed method offers a viable alternative to pure generative approaches for character animation, focusing on a more constrained and structured setup. The compatibility with physics engine also suggests that future work can explore more realistic physics simulation.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a worthwhile contribution with clear strengths in 3D consistency and integration with physics-based approaches, addressing a core problem in current text-to-4D generation. The novelty of the approach is decent, but building off existing video diffusion models. The main limitations are the reliance on an external text-to-3D system and the manual rigging process. The contribution is significant enough to advance the field, the reliance on external components and the manual rigging process means a higher score cannot be justified. If the paper had tackled the asset creation pipeline more holistically, or provided quantitative results with the physics simulations the score would be substantially higher. The work still showcases a promising direction for future research.

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Detecting PTSD in Clinical Interviews: A Comparative Analysis of NLP Methods and Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01216v1)
- **Authors**: Feng Chen, Dror Ben-Zeev, Gillian Sparks, Arya Kadakia, Trevor Cohen
- **Abstract**: Post-Traumatic Stress Disorder (PTSD) remains underdiagnosed in clinical settings, presenting opportunities for automated detection to identify patients. This study evaluates natural language processing approaches for detecting PTSD from clinical interview transcripts. We compared general and mental health-specific transformer models (BERT/RoBERTa), embedding-based methods (SentenceBERT/LLaMA), and large language model prompting strategies (zero-shot/few-shot/chain-of-thought) using the DAIC-WOZ dataset. Domain-specific models significantly outperformed general models (Mental-RoBERTa F1=0.643 vs. RoBERTa-base 0.485). LLaMA embeddings with neural networks achieved the highest performance (F1=0.700). Zero-shot prompting using DSM-5 criteria yielded competitive results without training data (F1=0.657). Performance varied significantly across symptom severity and comorbidity status, with higher accuracy for severe PTSD cases and patients with comorbid depression. Our findings highlight the potential of domain-adapted embeddings and LLMs for scalable screening while underscoring the need for improved detection of nuanced presentations and offering insights for developing clinically viable AI tools for PTSD assessment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the application of natural language processing (NLP) techniques, including large language models (LLMs), to detect Post-Traumatic Stress Disorder (PTSD) from clinical interview transcripts. The study compares the performance of general-purpose and mental health-specific transformer models (BERT/RoBERTa), embedding-based methods (SentenceBERT/LLaMA with a Deep Feed-Forward Neural Network (DFNN)), and various LLM prompting strategies (zero-shot, few-shot, and chain-of-thought). The DAIC-WOZ dataset is used for evaluation. The results indicate that domain-specific models outperform general models, LLaMA embeddings with DFNNs achieve the highest performance, and zero-shot prompting offers a competitive baseline without training data. The study also explores performance variations across symptom severity and comorbidity with depression. The findings highlight the potential of domain-adapted embeddings and LLMs for scalable PTSD screening.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has several aspects of novelty:

    *   It offers a comparative analysis of various NLP methods, including LLMs, for PTSD detection in clinical interview transcripts. While prior work has addressed PTSD detection, the comprehensive comparison across different approaches, especially LLM prompting strategies, is a significant contribution.
    *   The application of LLaMA, a relatively recent open-source LLM, to PTSD detection in a clinical setting is novel. Comparing LLaMA to more established transformer models like BERT and RoBERTa provides valuable insights into the benefits of increased model size and architecture for this task.
    *   The investigation into how model performance varies with PTSD symptom severity and comorbidity with depression is a valuable addition, as it addresses clinically relevant aspects of the problem.
    *   The application of zero-shot prompting using the DSM-5 criteria and subsequent evaluation of the accuracy and efficacy of the process is novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper has significant implications for advancing the development of automated PTSD screening tools.

    *   The demonstration that domain-specific models and LLaMA embeddings achieve superior performance over general-purpose models underscores the importance of domain adaptation and sophisticated model architectures for this task. This can guide future research in this area.
    *   The finding that zero-shot prompting with DSM-5 criteria provides a competitive baseline without training data is significant for low-resource settings where labeled data is scarce.
    *   The analysis of performance across symptom severity and comorbidity highlights the limitations of current approaches and the need for further research to address these challenges.
    *   The work provides insights into the potential of LLMs to leverage pre-trained knowledge and apply diagnostic criteria for psychiatric assessment, potentially streamlining the screening and early detection of PTSD in clinical settings.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Comprehensive comparison of multiple NLP methods, including LLMs.
    *   Use of a clinically relevant dataset (DAIC-WOZ).
    *   Investigation of performance across symptom severity and comorbidity.
    *   Rigorous evaluation metrics and statistical analysis.
    *   Clear and well-written presentation of results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The DAIC-WOZ dataset, while valuable, is a simulated clinical context. Real-world clinical interviews may present different challenges due to variations in interview styles, patient populations, and data quality.
    *   The chunking approach used to handle long transcripts might obscure temporal patterns that could be diagnostically relevant.
    *   The study uses a relatively old version of LLaMA. Using more recent versions may improve the results.
    *   While the prompts are well-designed, the zero-shot method may require further refinements on the prompt design and potentially additional diagnostic criterias.
    *   The generalizability of the findings to diverse clinical populations remains to be established. The population is limited, therefore it can be hard to claim if the processes would be effective on all patients.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on automated PTSD detection by providing a comprehensive benchmark and highlighting the potential of LLMs and domain-adapted models. The findings can guide the development of more effective and clinically viable screening tools. The study also points to important directions for future research, such as addressing challenges related to symptom severity, comorbidity, and the need for interpretable models.

**Score:** 7.5/10

**Justification:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by providing a comprehensive comparative analysis of NLP methods, including LLMs, for PTSD detection in clinical interview transcripts. The use of a clinically relevant dataset, investigation of performance across symptom severity and comorbidity, and rigorous evaluation metrics add to the paper's value. While the DAIC-WOZ dataset has limitations and the chunking approach might obscure temporal patterns, the paper's strengths outweigh these weaknesses. The study is likely to influence future research on automated PTSD detection and guide the development of more effective screening tools. The incorporation of the DSM-5 criteria, along with the exploration of zero-shot prompting, represents a novel approach that could streamline early PTSD detection. However, the results are based on one dataset. There is a need to conduct similar experiments on other relevant PTSD-detecting datasets to further justify the effectiveness of zero-shot learning.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompting Forgetting: Unlearning in GANs via Textual Guidance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01218v1)
- **Authors**: Piyush Nagasubramaniam, Neeraj Karamchandani, Chen Wu, Sencun Zhu
- **Abstract**: State-of-the-art generative models exhibit powerful image-generation capabilities, introducing various ethical and legal challenges to service providers hosting these models. Consequently, Content Removal Techniques (CRTs) have emerged as a growing area of research to control outputs without full-scale retraining. Recent work has explored the use of Machine Unlearning in generative models to address content removal. However, the focus of such research has been on diffusion models, and unlearning in Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) has remained largely unexplored. We address this gap by proposing Text-to-Unlearn, a novel framework that selectively unlearns concepts from pre-trained GANs using only text prompts, enabling feature unlearning, identity unlearning, and fine-grained tasks like expression and multi-attribute removal in models trained on human faces. Leveraging natural language descriptions, our approach guides the unlearning process without requiring additional datasets or supervised fine-tuning, offering a scalable and efficient solution. To evaluate its effectiveness, we introduce an automatic unlearning assessment method adapted from state-of-the-art image-text alignment metrics, providing a comprehensive analysis of the unlearning methodology. To our knowledge, Text-to-Unlearn is the first cross-modal unlearning framework for GANs, representing a flexible and efficient advancement in managing generative model behavior.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Text-to-Unlearn," a novel framework for unlearning concepts from pre-trained GANs using only textual prompts.  Unlike prior work focusing on diffusion models or requiring additional data/supervised fine-tuning for GANs, this approach leverages natural language descriptions to guide the unlearning process. The method enables feature unlearning, identity unlearning, and fine-grained tasks like expression and multi-attribute removal, even in models trained on complex datasets like FFHQ. It introduces an automatic unlearning assessment metric using Vision-Language Models (VLMs) for comprehensive evaluation. Text-to-Unlearn allows for targeted concept removal without additional datasets and can be used in tandem with latent mappers for post-unlearning image manipulation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the cross-modal unlearning framework for GANs. Prior work in GAN unlearning has often been limited to simplistic attribute erasure or has required supervised fine-tuning with annotated data. Using only text prompts to drive the unlearning process in GANs, a task that lacks a natural text input unlike diffusion models, is a significant contribution. The automatic unlearning assessment based on VLM image-text alignment metrics is also a novel contribution and helps to move away from the subjective visual evaluation often seen in GAN literature.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the practical challenges that arise when deploying generative models.  The ability to selectively remove concepts is critical for ethical AI development and legal compliance (e.g., GDPR).  Text-to-Unlearn offers a flexible and efficient solution to manage generative model behavior without incurring the costs of full retraining.  The fact that it works without curated unlearning datasets makes it more applicable in real-world scenarios where those datasets are unavailable or inaccessible due to privacy concerns.  The ability to unlearn fine-grained attributes and identities also expands the range of potential applications.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Text-guided approach:** Enables scalable and efficient unlearning without needing specific training data or complex manual annotations.
    *   **Fine-grained control:** Supports a variety of unlearning tasks, including feature removal, identity erasure, and attribute modification.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** Introduces a quantitative metric for assessing unlearning performance using VLMs.
    *   **Practical:** Addresses a real-world problem (content removal) with a practical, data-efficient solution.
    * Enables post unlearning edits to the underlying GAN model.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on CLIP (or other VLM):** The method's effectiveness is directly tied to the capabilities and biases of the pre-trained VLM used (CLIP). If the VLM has limitations or isn't well-aligned with the concepts to be unlearned, the approach might struggle. The authors acknowledge this limitation but it's important to highlight as it can inhibit broader applications.
    *   **Dependence on Latent Mapper quality:** The quality of the latent mapper directly influences the degree to which targeted features can be added or removed. If the latent space isn’t truly disentangled then it will limit the effectiveness of Text-to-Unlearn.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While the empirical results are promising, the paper lacks in-depth theoretical analysis that provides a robust mathematical foundation for the approach.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in generative unlearning and responsible AI.  It could stimulate further investigation into cross-modal approaches for controlling generative model behavior, especially for GANs.  The proposed evaluation metric could become a standard for assessing unlearning performance. It opens up new research avenues for debiasing VLMs and disentangling latent spaces. The ability to leverage GANs for image generation as opposed to diffusion models is another potential benefit.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The framework addresses a practically important problem in the responsible use of generative models. Although reliant on the CLIP model, the text-to-unlearn framework shows the unlearning of the targeted attributes while preserving the generative capability of GANs. The method is not without its limitations, the proposed method is a sound approach that has high potential for wider real-world applications.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and practically significant framework for unlearning in GANs using only text prompts. The empirical results demonstrate its effectiveness across various tasks, and the introduction of a VLM-based evaluation metric is a valuable contribution. However, the reliance on CLIP's capabilities and the relative lack of theoretical analysis hold it back from being considered a truly groundbreaking (9 or 10) contribution. The results provided are sound and the paper provides a good approach for unlearning specific concepts using GANs.
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### rPPG-SysDiaGAN: Systolic-Diastolic Feature Localization in rPPG Using Generative Adversarial Network with Multi-Domain Discriminator
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01220v1)
- **Authors**: Banafsheh Adami, Nima Karimian
- **Abstract**: Remote photoplethysmography (rPPG) offers a novel approach to noninvasive monitoring of vital signs, such as respiratory rate, utilizing a camera. Although several supervised and self-supervised methods have been proposed, they often fail to accurately reconstruct the PPG signal, particularly in distinguishing between systolic and diastolic components. Their primary focus tends to be solely on extracting heart rate, which may not accurately represent the complete PPG signal. To address this limitation, this paper proposes a novel deep learning architecture using Generative Adversarial Networks by introducing multi-discriminators to extract rPPG signals from facial videos. These discriminators focus on the time domain, the frequency domain, and the second derivative of the original time domain signal. The discriminator integrates four loss functions: variance loss to mitigate local minima caused by noise; dynamic time warping loss to address local minima induced by alignment and sequences of variable lengths; Sparsity Loss for heart rate adjustment, and Variance Loss to ensure a uniform distribution across the desired frequency domain and time interval between systolic and diastolic phases of the PPG signal.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the rPPG-SysDiaGAN paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel deep learning architecture called rPPG-SysDiaGAN for remote photoplethysmography (rPPG). This architecture uses a Generative Adversarial Network (GAN) with a multi-domain discriminator to extract rPPG signals from facial videos. The discriminator operates in the time domain, frequency domain, and the second derivative of the time domain signal, incorporating specific loss functions to enhance performance. These losses include: a variance loss to reduce noise, dynamic time warping (DTW) to address alignment issues, sparsity loss for heart rate adjustment, and a variance loss to ensure uniform distribution in the PPG signal's frequency domain. The method aims to improve the accuracy of rPPG signal reconstruction, especially in capturing systolic and diastolic components, rather than just focusing on heart rate.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Multi-Domain Approach:** Analyzing rPPG in the time, frequency, and second derivative domains is a sound strategy for capturing more comprehensive information about the signal. The incorporation of the second derivative, specifically to capture systolic and diastolic peaks, is a key differentiator from much prior work.
    *   **Multi-Discriminator GAN:** Using separate discriminators to focus on different aspects of the rPPG signal (time domain, frequency, second derivative) in the GAN training process is a valuable approach, and the specific loss functions tied to each domain appear logically chosen.
    *   **Comprehensive Loss Functions:**  The combination of variance loss, DTW loss, and sparsity loss seems well-suited to address the challenges of rPPG signal extraction. The inclusion of Soft-DTW addresses potential temporal misalignments between real and generated PPG signals, making the model more robust to variations in PPG waveform.
    *   **Extensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates the proposed method on several standard rPPG datasets using relevant metrics, including both heart rate accuracy and waveform similarity, which supports its claims.
    *   **Ablation Study:**  The ablation study provides insight into the contribution of each component, demonstrating the effectiveness of the different loss functions and architectural modifications, reinforcing the paper's claims.
    *   The paper demonstrates a solid improvement in measuring HR across different datasets, significantly outperforming several existing methods and the paper clearly demonstrates the value of their approach through well-presented empirical results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Supervised Learning Reliance:** The method is supervised, requiring ground truth PPG signals for training.  While this generally leads to higher accuracy than unsupervised approaches, it limits the applicability of the method in situations where ground truth data are unavailable.
    *   **Dependence on Facial Video Quality:** rPPG methods are susceptible to changes in lighting, head movement, and skin tone. Although the method strives to address these issues, its robustness to extreme variations should be explored further.
    *   **Limited Novelty in GAN Architecture:** While the combination of components is novel, the individual GAN building blocks are not especially innovative. The Swin-Attention U-Net architecture is combining existing components in the field and may not be groundbreaking in that respect.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Computational Cost:** The paper lacks a discussion on the computational complexity and real-time performance of the rPPG-SysDiaGAN, which is important for practical applications.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Enhanced rPPG Reconstruction:** The primary significance of the paper lies in its attempt to reconstruct the *entire* rPPG waveform, rather than simply estimating heart rate. This opens up possibilities for extracting more detailed physiological information.
    *   **Potential for Clinical Applications:** Accurate reconstruction of rPPG signals could lead to novel diagnostic tools for cardiovascular health, particularly in remote monitoring scenarios.
    *   **Contribution to Multi-Domain Signal Processing:** The paper serves as an example of how combining time, frequency, and derivative information can improve signal analysis, which has broader implications for other signal processing tasks.

*   **Critical Score:**

The paper presents a significant advancement in rPPG by going beyond simple heart rate estimation. The proposed multi-domain discriminator and associated loss functions are well-motivated and experimentally validated. While the reliance on supervised learning and the utilization of existing GAN components slightly reduces its novelty, the performance gains and the focus on waveform reconstruction warrant a high rating. The paper has clear potential for clinical translation and further research.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Resilient Federated Learning in CyberEdge Networks: Recent Advances and Future Trends
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01240v1)
- **Authors**: Kai Li, Zhengyang Zhang, Azadeh Pourkabirian, Wei Ni, Falko Dressler, Ozgur B. Akan
- **Abstract**: In this survey, we investigate the most recent techniques of resilient federated learning (ResFL) in CyberEdge networks, focusing on joint training with agglomerative deduction and feature-oriented security mechanisms. We explore adaptive hierarchical learning strategies to tackle non-IID data challenges, improving scalability and reducing communication overhead. Fault tolerance techniques and agglomerative deduction mechanisms are studied to detect unreliable devices, refine model updates, and enhance convergence stability. Unlike existing FL security research, we comprehensively analyze feature-oriented threats, such as poisoning, inference, and reconstruction attacks that exploit model features. Moreover, we examine resilient aggregation techniques, anomaly detection, and cryptographic defenses, including differential privacy and secure multi-party computation, to strengthen FL security. In addition, we discuss the integration of 6G, large language models (LLMs), and interoperable learning frameworks to enhance privacy-preserving and decentralized cross-domain training. These advancements offer ultra-low latency, artificial intelligence (AI)-driven network management, and improved resilience against adversarial attacks, fostering the deployment of secure ResFL in CyberEdge networks.
- **Summary**: This survey paper explores resilient federated learning (ResFL) techniques in CyberEdge networks, focusing on joint training with agglomerative deduction and feature-oriented security mechanisms. It covers adaptive hierarchical learning for non-IID data, fault tolerance, and anomaly detection. The paper analyzes feature-oriented threats like poisoning and inference attacks, and examines resilient aggregation, anomaly detection, and cryptographic defenses. The survey also looks at integrating 6G, large language models (LLMs), and interoperable learning frameworks. Ultimately, the survey aims to improve resilience, reduce latency, and enhance network management through AI in CyberEdge networks.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper attempts to provide a comprehensive overview of resilient federated learning in the specific context of CyberEdge networks, a relevant area due to the increasing importance of edge computing and the unique challenges presented by IoT and Metaverse applications. The survey covers several emerging techniques and provides a good overview of recent trends, including the integration of 6G and LLMs. The inclusion of feature-oriented threats and defenses provides a more nuanced perspective on security challenges beyond traditional poisoning attacks.

However, several limitations prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. While the paper compiles existing techniques, it lacks deep theoretical insights and doesn't propose any novel algorithms or frameworks. Many of the techniques discussed, such as differential privacy and secure multi-party computation, are well-established in the broader federated learning literature and their application to CyberEdge networks is fairly straightforward. Moreover, the future research directions, while relevant, are somewhat generic and don't provide concrete research agendas. Also, it references papers as recent as 2025, which is impossible (as it's currently only April 2024), indicating speculative or fabricated information.

The level of critical analysis of the surveyed techniques also feels lacking. A stronger survey would not just describe methods but also compare them rigorously based on specific metrics applicable to CyberEdge networks (e.g., latency, energy efficiency, communication overhead under specific network conditions).

**Strengths:**

*   Comprehensive coverage of ResFL techniques.
*   Focus on CyberEdge networks, a relevant and growing field.
*   Discussion of feature-oriented threats beyond traditional attacks.
*   Includes recent trends like 6G and LLM integration.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Lacks novel algorithms or frameworks.
*   Limited critical analysis and comparison of surveyed techniques.
*   Future research directions are somewhat generic.
*   References non-existent papers from the future.

**Justification:**

While the survey provides a useful overview of existing techniques and identifies relevant future directions, it doesn't offer substantial new insights or original contributions. The lack of in-depth analysis and the speculative references significantly impact its credibility and overall value. For a survey paper to be truly impactful, it needs to offer more than just a compilation of existing work; it needs to provide a critical assessment, identify key challenges, and suggest concrete research avenues. The premature or fabricated reference dates decrease the score significantly.
