### Catastrophic Forgetting in LLMs: A Comparative Analysis Across Language Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01241v1)
- **Authors**: Naimul Haque
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have significantly advanced Natural Language Processing (NLP), particularly in Natural Language Understanding (NLU) tasks. As we progress toward an agentic world where LLM-based agents autonomously handle specialized tasks, it becomes crucial for these models to adapt to new tasks without forgetting previously learned information - a challenge known as catastrophic forgetting. This study evaluates the continual fine-tuning of various open-source LLMs with different parameter sizes (specifically models under 10 billion parameters) on key NLU tasks from the GLUE benchmark, including SST-2, MRPC, CoLA, and MNLI. By employing prompt engineering and task-specific adjustments, we assess and compare the models' abilities to retain prior knowledge while learning new tasks. Our results indicate that models such as Phi-3.5-mini exhibit minimal forgetting while maintaining strong learning capabilities, making them well-suited for continual learning environments. Additionally, models like Orca-2-7b and Qwen2.5-7B demonstrate impressive learning abilities and overall performance after fine-tuning. This work contributes to understanding catastrophic forgetting in LLMs and highlights prompting engineering to optimize model performance for continual learning scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates catastrophic forgetting in Large Language Models (LLMs) during continual fine-tuning on Natural Language Understanding (NLU) tasks. It evaluates several open-source LLMs (under 10B parameters) on GLUE benchmark tasks (SST-2, MRPC, COLA, MNLI), using prompt engineering and task-specific adjustments. The study aims to identify models that exhibit minimal forgetting while maintaining strong learning capabilities. The findings suggest that models like Phi-3.5-mini demonstrate minimal forgetting, while models like Orca-2-7b and Qwen2.5-7B show impressive learning abilities. The paper highlights the importance of prompt engineering in optimizing model performance in continual learning scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty is moderate. Catastrophic forgetting in LLMs is a known problem. Continual fine-tuning and prompt engineering are also established techniques. The specific contribution lies in the comparative analysis of *specific* open-source models (under 10B parameters) on a *specific* set of NLU tasks from the GLUE benchmark and the specific techniques used for prompt engineering. While the problem is not new, the experimentation with and comparative analysis of the specific models and techniques is a useful empirical contribution. A weak point is the limited exploration of continual learning strategies beyond sequential fine-tuning. It lacks exploration of more advanced techniques like memory replay, regularization methods, or dynamic architectures.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance is moderate. The results provide practical insights for researchers and practitioners working with smaller LLMs in continual learning environments. Identifying models that exhibit a good trade-off between learning and forgetting is valuable for building LLM-based agents that can adapt to new tasks without losing previously learned knowledge. However, the paper doesn't introduce groundbreaking theoretical advancements or novel methodologies. The focus on smaller models is beneficial, as they are more accessible and computationally feasible for many researchers. However, the analysis of specific prompt engineering strategies is shallow and could be improved by providing more detail. The analysis relies heavily on accuracy as the only metric, not exploring other performance aspects. Also, there is limited generalization since all tasks are from the same benchmark.

* **Strengths:**
    * Comprehensive empirical evaluation of various open-source LLMs.
    * Focus on smaller models (under 10B parameters) making results accessible.
    * Clear presentation of results and findings.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * Limited methodological novelty.
    * Restricted task scope (only GLUE benchmark).
    * Shallow analysis of prompt engineering strategies.
    * Dependence on a single evaluation metric (accuracy).
    * Lack of exploration of advanced continual learning techniques.
    * Limited discussion on computational costs and resource constraints associated with continual fine-tuning in practical deployments.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence practitioners by providing empirical guidance on selecting and fine-tuning smaller LLMs for continual learning tasks. It may also inspire further research into more effective continual learning strategies for LLMs. However, it lacks the potential to fundamentally reshape the field due to its limited novelty and scope.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:** The paper provides a useful empirical study of catastrophic forgetting in LLMs and contributes to the understanding of how different models perform under continual fine-tuning.  However, it lacks substantial novelty in methodology or findings. It focuses on known techniques applied to a specific set of models and tasks. The lack of exploration of more advanced continual learning strategies, computational costs, and more thorough analysis holds the paper back from achieving a higher score. While practically relevant, it is not a transformative contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Automated Factual Benchmarking for In-Car Conversational Systems using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01248v1)
- **Authors**: Rafael Giebisch, Ken E. Friedl, Lev Sorokin, Andrea Stocco
- **Abstract**: In-car conversational systems bring the promise to improve the in-vehicle user experience. Modern conversational systems are based on Large Language Models (LLMs), which makes them prone to errors such as hallucinations, i.e., inaccurate, fictitious, and therefore factually incorrect information. In this paper, we present an LLM-based methodology for the automatic factual benchmarking of in-car conversational systems. We instantiate our methodology with five LLM-based methods, leveraging ensembling techniques and diverse personae to enhance agreement and minimize hallucinations. We use our methodology to evaluate CarExpert, an in-car retrieval-augmented conversational question answering system, with respect to the factual correctness to a vehicle's manual. We produced a novel dataset specifically created for the in-car domain, and tested our methodology against an expert evaluation. Our results show that the combination of GPT-4 with the Input Output Prompting achieves over 90 per cent factual correctness agreement rate with expert evaluations, other than being the most efficient approach yielding an average response time of 4.5s. Our findings suggest that LLM-based testing constitutes a viable approach for the validation of conversational systems regarding their factual correctness.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Automated Factual Benchmarking for In-Car Conversational Systems using Large Language Models."

**Summary**

The paper introduces a methodology for automatically benchmarking the factual correctness of in-car conversational systems that leverage Large Language Models (LLMs). The core idea is to use other LLMs as judges to assess the factual consistency and relevance of the in-car system's responses against a ground truth dataset derived from the vehicle's owner's manual. The authors experiment with five different LLM-based methods, including input-output prompting, chain-of-thought, self-consistency, multi-persona self-collaboration, and a round table conference approach (ensemble). They evaluate their methodology using CarExpert, an in-car conversational system developed at BMW.  The results show that a combination of GPT-4 with input/output prompting achieves high agreement with expert evaluations, while also maintaining efficiency. The paper argues that LLM-based testing offers a viable path for validating the factual accuracy of conversational systems.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addressing a Relevant Problem:** Factual correctness is a crucial requirement for in-car conversational systems. The paper directly addresses a practical problem that is often difficult and time-consuming to solve manually.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper outlines a clear and well-defined methodology, including dataset creation, prompt design, and evaluation metrics.
    *   **Comprehensive Experimentation:** The authors conducted extensive experiments by testing various LLMs, reasoning methods, and temperature settings. This systematic approach allows for a comprehensive evaluation of the methodology.
    *   **Novel Dataset:** The creation of a domain-specific dataset tailored for in-car conversational systems is a significant contribution. This dataset is invaluable for future research in this area.
    *   **Comparative Analysis:** The paper offers a comparative analysis of different LLM-based methods, providing valuable insights into their strengths and weaknesses.
    *   **Efficiency Analysis**: By measuring time per token and overall execution time, the study considers the practical deployment aspect of the proposed benchmarking framework.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses on a specific in-car system (CarExpert) and a single vehicle manual. Generalizing the findings to other systems and domains might require further investigation. The paper acknowledges this.
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The methodology's reliance on LLMs to judge the factual correctness means that it's susceptible to the biases and limitations inherent in those LLMs. While the paper attempts to mitigate these issues through techniques like ensembling, the possibility of inaccuracies remains.
    *   **Evaluation Metric Limitations:** The sole use of accuracy (agreement between LLM judges and experts) can be misleading, as it doesn't capture the nuances of correctness. A more granular analysis of different types of errors could provide a richer understanding of the methodology's performance.  Qualitative analysis addresses these limitations but doesn't provide a quantifiable measure.
    *   **Lack of Public Availability:** The study uses proprietary LLMs and a non-public dataset which would hinder the reproducibility by other researchers. While this is common in industrial research, its important to address the limits of the study.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The paper combines existing techniques in a novel way to address a specific problem in the in-car conversational AI domain. While using LLMs for evaluation has been explored, the application to factual correctness in this context, with a curated dataset and a systematic comparison of methods, is a valuable contribution.
    *   The identification of different error types (confusion of terminology, hallucinations, common sense errors) contributes to a better understanding of the challenges in building factual conversational systems.
    *   The paper could potentially influence the development and testing practices of in-car conversational systems, leading to improved safety and user experience.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, the paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field, but isn't without limitations. While the approach is practical and well-executed, the dependency on specific LLMs and the limitations of the evaluation metric somewhat limit its generalizability. Also the study fails to share the code and dataset to allow other researchers to reproduce the work.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Plan-and-Act using Large Language Models for Interactive Agreement
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01252v1)
- **Authors**: Kazuhiro Sasabuchi, Naoki Wake, Atsushi Kanehira, Jun Takamatsu, Katsushi Ikeuchi
- **Abstract**: Recent large language models (LLMs) are capable of planning robot actions. In this paper, we explore how LLMs can be used for planning actions with tasks involving situational human-robot interaction (HRI). A key problem of applying LLMs in situational HRI is balancing between "respecting the current human's activity" and "prioritizing the robot's task," as well as understanding the timing of when to use the LLM to generate an action plan. In this paper, we propose a necessary plan-and-act skill design to solve the above problems. We show that a critical factor for enabling a robot to switch between passive / active interaction behavior is to provide the LLM with an action text about the current robot's action. We also show that a second-stage question to the LLM (about the next timing to call the LLM) is necessary for planning actions at an appropriate timing. The skill design is applied to an Engage skill and is tested on four distinct interaction scenarios. We show that by using the skill design, LLMs can be leveraged to easily scale to different HRI scenarios with a reasonable success rate reaching 90% on the test scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a skill design for human-robot interaction (HRI) that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) for runtime planning and acting. The approach aims to address the challenge of balancing the robot's task goals with the need to respect the human's current activities. The key components of the skill design include: (1) a bottom-up action set, (2) an event manager that uses LLMs for timing decisions (when to generate the next action plan), and (3) the use of action texts (descriptions of the robot's current action) to provide context to the LLM and improve its performance. The approach is evaluated in simulation across four different HRI scenarios, demonstrating a reasonable success rate. The experiments highlight the importance of the action text in enabling the robot to prioritize its task when appropriate.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the specific integration of LLMs for runtime HRI planning, incorporating the concepts of action texts and a two-stage question approach to manage timing. While LLMs have been used in robotics before (including for offline planning and HRI), the focus on *situational* runtime planning with a specific emphasis on balancing robot goals and human activities distinguishes this work. The bottom-up action set is a practical engineering touch but not inherently novel from a research perspective. The idea of using LLMs to generate actions for robots in HRI scenarios is not new, however, the careful consideration of *when* to call the LLM and *what information to provide* to the LLM to achieve appropriate HRI behavior is a significant contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper contributes to the growing body of research on LLMs in robotics, particularly in the context of HRI. It offers a concrete skill design that addresses a common challenge in HRI – adapting to dynamic human behavior. The experimental results, while limited to simulation, provide evidence that the proposed approach can achieve reasonable success rates in different interaction scenarios. By enabling robots to reason about human activities and their own goals at runtime, this work moves closer to more natural and adaptive human-robot collaboration.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clearly Defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of balancing robot goals and human activities in situational HRI.
    *   **Well-Structured Design:** The skill design is well-structured and the rationale behind each component (action set, event manager, action text) is clearly explained.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The experimental results provide evidence that the proposed approach can achieve reasonable success rates.
    *   **Practical Contributions:** The paper offers insights and practical techniques for integrating LLMs into HRI systems. The discussion of the "bottom-up" action set and the timing questions contributes to making the use of LLMs tractable within robotic systems.
    *   **Reuseable Ecosystem**: The paper highlights reuseability due to the skill being based on a framework, this allows for quick integration into existing robot designs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is limited to simulation and a single skill (engage). The generalization of the approach to more complex HRI tasks and real-world environments remains to be seen.
    *   **Dependency on LLM Performance:** The performance of the approach is heavily dependent on the capabilities of the LLM. The paper acknowledges this but does not fully address the issue of robustness to LLM errors or biases.
    *   **Hand-Crafted Elements:**  While the approach leverages LLMs, there are still several hand-crafted elements, such as the prompts used by the LLM and the specific actions in the action set. The paper mentions the reliance on reference responses and questions whether the design is too reliant on guidance to the LLMs, and discusses it's own skill based approach is sufficient to prioritize it's task.
    *   **Limited comparison:** While compared to not adding action text, there is no comparison made with other research that has already applied the knowledge of respecting the human's current activity.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the design of future HRI systems that leverage LLMs. The concept of action texts and the two-stage question approach could be adopted by other researchers and practitioners. The focus on situational runtime planning is also likely to become increasingly important as robots are deployed in more dynamic and unpredictable environments.

**Justification for Score:**

Based on the above analysis, the paper demonstrates a **moderate** level of novelty and significance. It presents a well-structured skill design for a relevant problem in HRI, and provides experimental evidence that the approach can achieve reasonable success rates. However, the limitations in scope and the dependency on LLM performance temper its overall impact. The paper could become more influential with more real-world validation and a deeper analysis of the trade-offs between LLM-driven planning and hand-crafted rules. Despite these limitations, the paper offers valuable insights into integrating LLMs into HRI systems and contributes to the growing body of research in this area.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 6/10

### Grade Guard: A Smart System for Short Answer Automated Grading
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01253v1)
- **Authors**: Niharika Dadu, Harsh Vardhan Singh, Romi Banerjee
- **Abstract**: The advent of large language models (LLMs) in the education sector has provided impetus to automate grading short answer questions. LLMs make evaluating short answers very efficient, thus addressing issues like staff shortage. However, in the task of Automated Short Answer Grading (ASAG), LLM responses are influenced by diverse perspectives in their training dataset, leading to inaccuracies in evaluating nuanced or partially correct answers. To address this challenge, we propose a novel framework, Grade Guard. 1. To enhance the task-based specialization of the LLMs, the temperature parameter has been fine-tuned using Root Mean Square Error (RMSE). 2. Unlike traditional approaches, LLMs in Grade Guard compute an Indecisiveness Score (IS) along with the grade to reflect uncertainty in predicted grades. 3. Introduced Confidence-Aware Loss (CAL) to generate an optimized Indecisiveness Score (IS). 4. To improve reliability, self-reflection based on the optimized IS has been introduced into the framework, enabling human re-evaluation to minimize incorrect grade assignments. Our experimentation shows that the best setting of Grade Guard outperforms traditional methods by 19.16% RMSE in Upstage Solar Pro, 23.64% RMSE in Upstage Solar Mini, 4.00% RMSE in Gemini 1.5 Flash, and 10.20% RMSE in GPT 4-o Mini. Future work includes improving interpretability by generating rationales for grades to enhance accuracy. Expanding benchmark datasets and annotating them with domain-specific nuances will enhance grading accuracy. Finally, analyzing feedback to enhance confidence in predicted grades, reduce biases, optimize grading criteria, and personalize learning while supporting multilingual grading systems will make the solution more accurate, adaptable, fair, and inclusive.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Grade Guard," a novel framework designed to improve the reliability of large language models (LLMs) in automated short answer grading (ASAG). The framework addresses the inconsistencies and inaccuracies observed when using LLMs directly for grading by incorporating several key modules: a Creativity Regulator Module (CRM) for fine-tuning the temperature parameter, an Indecisiveness Regulator Module (IRM) that introduces a Confidence-Aware Loss (CAL) to penalize indecisiveness, and a Self-Reflective Grader Module (SRGM) that flags uncertain predictions for human review. The paper details the implementation, dataset preparation, prompt engineering, and experimental setup. The results show that Grade Guard outperforms traditional LLMs in reducing RMSE and misclassifications, particularly with Upstage Solar Pro and Upstage Solar Mini models. The paper concludes by discussing potential areas for improvement and future research directions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the use of LLMs for ASAG is not entirely new, the specific combination of techniques within the Grade Guard framework demonstrates some novelty. The introduction of the Indecisiveness Score (IS) and Confidence-Aware Loss (CAL) to explicitly address LLM uncertainty is a significant contribution. The self-reflection mechanism to flag potentially incorrect grading decisions for human review adds another layer of reliability.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in applying LLMs to education: ensuring the accuracy and reliability of automated grading. The described framework helps improve the dependability of LLMs in ASAG. The results, showing a considerable reduction in RMSE and misclassification, suggest the potential for practical applications in reducing teacher workload and improving grading efficiency. The performance gains are primarily concentrated on the Upstage Models. This suggests that GradeGuard might be particularly effective for models with specific characteristics. Further exploration into why certain models benefit more from the framework is required.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and motivation.
    *   Well-defined framework with distinct modules.
    *   Detailed explanation of the methodology and experimental setup.
    *   Empirical results demonstrating the effectiveness of Grade Guard.
    *   Identified potential areas for improvement and future research directions.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Dataset:** The experiments are primarily conducted on a single dataset (Mohler's dataset), which raises concerns about generalizability. Expanding the evaluation to include other benchmark datasets and domain-specific datasets is crucial.
    *   **Reliance on Proprietary LLMs:** The framework depends on proprietary LLMs like GPT-4-o-mini and Gemini. This can limit reproducibility and accessibility. Evaluating Grade Guard with open-source LLMs would increase its practical value.
    *   **Model Specific Gains**: The model shows disproportionately larger gains in a specific subset of LLMs. A deeper dive into the factors that causes the performance improvements is needed.
    *   **Limited interpretability:** The system flags instances for review, but doesn't provide detailed insight into the reasoning. Adding a justification of the score would greatly enhance usability.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more reliable and robust ASAG systems based on LLMs. The concepts of IS, CAL, and self-reflection could be adopted and extended by other researchers in the field. By addressing the problem of LLM uncertainty, the paper contributes to increasing trust and acceptance of automated grading technologies in education.

*   **Rigor:** The paper provides quantitative results with RMSE comparisons. Statistical significance testing would greatly improve the rigor of the study and demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach more conclusively.

**Score: 6**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel framework for improving the reliability of LLMs in ASAG, contributing a combination of IS, CAL and self-reflection, demonstrating improvements in grading accuracy. The improvements mainly concentrate on a subset of LLMs. It addresses a significant problem in the educational domain. However, the limited dataset and reliance on proprietary LLMs slightly constrain the paper's immediate practical impact and generalizability. While the results are promising, broader evaluations are needed. Statistical validation should be provided to more rigorously ascertain the robustness of the presented approach. Therefore a score of 6 is appropriate.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Facilitating Instructors-LLM Collaboration for Problem Design in Introductory Programming Classrooms
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01259v1)
- **Authors**: Muntasir Hoq, Jessica Vandenberg, Shuyin Jiao, Seung Lee, Bradford Mott, Narges Norouzi, James Lester, Bita Akram
- **Abstract**: Advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs), such as ChatGPT, offer significant opportunities to enhance instructional support in introductory programming courses. While extensive research has explored the effectiveness of LLMs in supporting student learning, limited studies have examined how these models can assist instructors in designing instructional activities. This work investigates how instructors' expertise in effective activity design can be integrated with LLMs' ability to generate novel and targeted programming problems, facilitating more effective activity creation for programming classrooms. To achieve this, we employ a participatory design approach to develop an instructor-authoring tool that incorporates LLM support, fostering collaboration between instructors and AI in generating programming exercises. This tool also allows instructors to specify common student mistakes and misconceptions, which informs the adaptive feedback generation process. We conduct case studies with three instructors, analyzing how they use our system to design programming problems for their introductory courses. Through these case studies, we assess instructors' perceptions of the usefulness and limitations of LLMs in authoring problem statements for instructional purposes. Additionally, we compare the efficiency, quality, effectiveness, and coverage of designed activities when instructors create problems with and without structured LLM prompting guidelines. Our findings provide insights into the potential of LLMs in enhancing instructor workflows and improving programming education and provide guidelines for designing effective AI-assisted problem-authoring interfaces.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Facilitating Instructors-LLM Collaboration for Problem Design in Introductory Programming Classrooms":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how instructors and Large Language Models (LLMs) like ChatGPT can collaborate to design programming problems for introductory computer science (CS1) courses. It introduces an instructor-authoring tool integrated within the INSIGHT classroom assistant, allowing instructors to specify common student mistakes/misconceptions to inform adaptive feedback generation. The study uses a participatory design approach and case studies with three CS1 instructors. The researchers analyze the instructors' perceptions of the usefulness and limitations of LLMs, compare the efficiency and quality of activity design with and without structured LLM prompting, and assess instructors' trust and confidence in integrating LLM-generated content. The findings offer insights into enhancing instructor workflows and improving programming education through AI-assisted problem authoring. Key research questions focused on instructors' perceptions of LLMs, their evaluation of LLM-generated outputs (problem statements, solutions, feedback), and their trust/confidence in integrating these outputs into their practices. The approach involved both unguided (instructors using LLMs independently) and guided (using scaffolded prompts from the researchers) design phases.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its focus on *instructor*-LLM collaboration for *problem design*, rather than the more common area of student-LLM interaction for learning and problem-solving. While there's existing work on using LLMs to generate educational content (exercises, worked examples, etc.), the specific exploration of how LLMs can augment and improve the *instructor's* design process is a valuable contribution. Also, the introduction of a tool that integrates the specification of student misconceptions into feedback generation, driven by LLMs, is a worthwhile step.
* **Significance:** The study addresses a critical need in CS1 education: creating effective and engaging programming problems.  Automating the problem design process can save instructor time and potentially improve the diversity and quality of the problems students are exposed to. If LLMs can effectively generate realistic student errors and relevant, targeted feedback, this can improve the educational value of the assignments.
* **Strengths:**
    *   **Focus on Instructor Perspective:** The paper successfully captures the instructors' perceptions, experiences, and concerns about using LLMs, which provides valuable qualitative data.
    *   **Guided vs. Unguided Comparison:** The comparison between the "unguided" and "guided" approaches provides a structured way to assess the impact of different levels of LLM support. It shows how guided prompts can improve efficiency and alignment with learning objectives while recognizing that creativity may be somewhat diminished.
    *   **Identification of Key Challenges:** The study highlights important limitations of LLMs, such as the need for human oversight, concerns about accuracy and appropriateness, and potential issues with over-detailing feedback.
    *   **INSIGHT Tool:** The INSIGHT Tool described seems promising as an interface to facilitate this collaboration, though it is not the primary focus of the analysis.
* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Small Sample Size:** The most significant limitation is the small sample size (three instructors). While the qualitative data is rich, it's difficult to generalize the findings to a broader population of CS1 instructors.
    *   **Limited Scope of CS1:**  The study focuses on introductory courses, and it is not clear whether the findings would transfer to more advanced courses.
    *   **Lack of Quantitative Evaluation:** While the qualitative analysis is insightful, the paper would be strengthened by some quantitative measures. For example, comparing student performance/engagement on problems designed with and without LLM assistance could provide more concrete evidence of the impact.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Misconception Tagging:** The description of how the misconception tagging system works is interesting, but more details on its design and evaluation would have been beneficial. How effective are the tagged LLM-generated solutions in mirroring the specified common misconceptions?
    *   **Limited discussion on how the instructors interact with the LLM tool itself:** There is a lack of information on how instructors can modify the LLM-generated exercises/solutions/feedback to align with their teaching styles.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper could influence the design of future AI-assisted tools for educators. By highlighting the benefits and limitations of LLMs, it can guide the development of systems that are more effective, reliable, and pedagogically sound. It also raises important questions about the role of AI in education and the need for human oversight.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, I would assign a score of **6.5**. The paper makes a valuable contribution by focusing on the relatively unexplored area of instructor-LLM collaboration for problem design. The qualitative analysis is insightful, and the "guided vs. unguided" comparison provides a useful framework for understanding the impact of different levels of LLM support. However, the small sample size and lack of quantitative evaluation limit the generalizability and impact of the findings. While the core idea is relatively novel, there could be further exploration in the misconception tagging area and how the tool itself impacts the workflow of instructors. Therefore a high score is difficult to justify, but the novel and interesting idea, with insightful findings merit this as a strong addition to the field.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 6/10

### Strategize Globally, Adapt Locally: A Multi-Turn Red Teaming Agent with Dual-Level Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01278v1)
- **Authors**: Si Chen, Xiao Yu, Ninareh Mehrabi, Rahul Gupta, Zhou Yu, Ruoxi Jia
- **Abstract**: The exploitation of large language models (LLMs) for malicious purposes poses significant security risks as these models become more powerful and widespread. While most existing red-teaming frameworks focus on single-turn attacks, real-world adversaries typically operate in multi-turn scenarios, iteratively probing for vulnerabilities and adapting their prompts based on threat model responses. In this paper, we propose \AlgName, a novel multi-turn red-teaming agent that emulates sophisticated human attackers through complementary learning dimensions: global tactic-wise learning that accumulates knowledge over time and generalizes to new attack goals, and local prompt-wise learning that refines implementations for specific goals when initial attempts fail. Unlike previous multi-turn approaches that rely on fixed strategy sets, \AlgName enables the agent to identify new jailbreak tactics, develop a goal-based tactic selection framework, and refine prompt formulations for selected tactics. Empirical evaluations on JailbreakBench demonstrate our framework's superior performance, achieving over 90\% attack success rates against GPT-3.5-Turbo and Llama-3.1-70B within 5 conversation turns, outperforming state-of-the-art baselines. These results highlight the effectiveness of dynamic learning in identifying and exploiting model vulnerabilities in realistic multi-turn scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GALA, a novel multi-turn red teaming agent designed to identify vulnerabilities in large language models (LLMs).  Unlike existing red-teaming frameworks, which often focus on single-turn attacks or predefined strategy sets, GALA learns adaptively through two complementary dimensions: global tactic-wise learning (discovering new attack tactics and building a goal-based selection framework) and local prompt-wise learning (refining prompt formulations for specific goals). The agent leverages a specialized memory module for inductive learning, adaptive planning, and efficient belief state tracking. Experimental results on JailbreakBench demonstrate that GALA achieves superior attack success rates and prompt diversity compared to state-of-the-art baselines, highlighting the effectiveness of dynamic learning in identifying and exploiting model vulnerabilities in realistic multi-turn scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:* The paper presents a genuinely novel approach to automated red teaming, addressing a crucial gap in the field.  The dual-learning framework, particularly the ability to discover new attack tactics *during* the red-teaming process, is a significant advancement over methods that rely on fixed strategy sets. The combination of global tactic learning and local prompt refinement is a powerful and elegant design.

*Significance:*  The work has significant practical and theoretical implications.  The high cost of human-led red teaming makes automated solutions vital for comprehensive LLM safety evaluations.  GALA offers a cost-effective and more diverse means of stress-testing LLMs compared to existing methods. Identifying vulnerabilities is crucial for hardening LLMs against malicious use cases. Also, the framework's success suggests that equipping LLMs with long-term memory and learning mechanisms could improve a range of other prompting related tasks.

*Strengths:*
*   The dual-learning framework is well-motivated and effectively implemented.
*   The experimental results convincingly demonstrate GALA's superior performance compared to strong baselines across diverse target models and misuse categories.
*   The analysis of the impact of different learning capability levels and attacker model capabilities provides valuable insights.
*   The qualitative results provide compelling examples of how GALA discovers new tactics and adapts to target model responses.
*   The paper is well-written and clearly presents the problem, approach, and results.

*Weaknesses:*

*   The paper does a commendable job of defining terms and the overall workflow; however, the explanation of the belief update mechanism could be more detailed. Exactly how the analysis of the responses impacts tactic and next prompt selection in an iterative way is not clear.
*   The reliance on a separate LLM for attack planning raises questions about the computational cost and the potential for cascading failures (if the attacker model is also vulnerable). Although the paper does provide results related to using a weaker attacker model; however, it does not address the cascading issue.
*   While the experiments use JailbreakBench, further evaluation on more realistic, complex scenarios would strengthen the paper's claims. It would be beneficial to see how GALA performs against LLMs with active defenses.
*   The reliance on prompt engineering for both the attacker and the evaluator introduces a degree of subjectivity, though they do try to mitigate this.

*Potential Influence:*

The paper is likely to have a significant influence on the field of LLM safety and red teaming.  The novel dual-learning framework and the demonstrated improvements in attack success rate and diversity will inspire further research in this area. GALA's ability to discover new attack tactics is particularly valuable and is a clear direction for future work. The approach's effectiveness may also lead to the development of more robust defenses that learn from adversarial interactions.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a significant and novel contribution to the field of LLM red teaming.  GALA's dual-learning framework addresses a critical gap in existing methods, and the experimental results convincingly demonstrate its superior performance and diversity. While there are some limitations related to complexity of the framework, the computational cost of using a weaker attacker, and evaluation on limited scenarios, the overall impact of the paper is substantial. The ability to learn new tactics and adapt to target model responses sets a new benchmark for automated red teaming and offers a clear path for future research.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Scaling Test-Time Inference with Policy-Optimized, Dynamic Retrieval-Augmented Generation via KV Caching and Decoding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01281v1)
- **Authors**: Sakhinana Sagar Srinivas, Venkataramana Runkana
- **Abstract**: We present a comprehensive framework for enhancing Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems through dynamic retrieval strategies and reinforcement fine-tuning. This approach significantly improves large language models on knowledge-intensive tasks, including opendomain question answering and complex reasoning. Our framework integrates two complementary techniques: Policy-Optimized RetrievalAugmented Generation (PORAG), which optimizes the use of retrieved information, and Adaptive Token-Layer Attention Scoring (ATLAS), which dynamically determines retrieval timing and content based on contextual needs. Together, these techniques enhance both the utilization and relevance of retrieved content, improving factual accuracy and response quality. Designed as a lightweight solution compatible with any Transformer-based LLM without requiring additional training, our framework excels in knowledge-intensive tasks, boosting output accuracy in RAG settings. We further propose CRITIC, a novel method to selectively compress key-value caches by token importance, mitigating memory bottlenecks in long-context applications. The framework also incorporates test-time scaling techniques to dynamically balance reasoning depth and computational resources, alongside optimized decoding strategies for faster inference. Experiments on benchmark datasets show that our framework reduces hallucinations, strengthens domain-specific reasoning, and achieves significant efficiency and scalability gains over traditional RAG systems. This integrated approach advances the development of robust, efficient, and scalable RAG systems across diverse applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a comprehensive framework for enhancing Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems. It combines Policy-Optimized Retrieval-Augmented Generation (PORAG) for optimizing the use of retrieved information, Adaptive Token-Layer Attention Scoring (ATLAS) for dynamically determining retrieval timing and content based on context, and CRITIC, a method to selectively compress key-value caches by token importance to mitigate memory bottlenecks. The framework also incorporates test-time scaling techniques and optimized decoding strategies for faster inference. Experiments demonstrate improved factual accuracy, domain-specific reasoning, efficiency, and scalability compared to traditional RAG systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the integration of several techniques rather than a breakthrough in any single area. The combination of policy optimization for RAG, adaptive retrieval timing based on attention gradients, and token-based KV cache compression is a novel approach. While each of these elements has precedents in the literature (e.g., policy optimization, adaptive attention, KV caching), their synergistic combination and application to RAG systems appear unique. Specifically, the dual reward mechanism in PORAG to balance retrieval fidelity and response quality and the weighted hybrid approach in CRITIC to determine token importance are worth noting.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses significant challenges in RAG systems, including balancing retrieval fidelity with response quality, dynamic retrieval, and computational efficiency. Successfully addressing these issues could have a significant impact on the scalability and practicality of RAG systems in diverse applications, particularly those requiring long contexts and real-time inference. The comprehensive experimental validation across three benchmarks with ablation studies and comparisons against baselines strengthens the paper's claims. Additionally, their effort in providing both theoretical justifications and analytical insights make the work stronger.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The paper presents a holistic approach that addresses multiple key challenges in RAG.
    *   **Synergistic Integration:** The framework combines several existing techniques in a novel and synergistic way.
    *   **Practical Considerations:** The paper includes practical optimizations like delayed compression activation and memory-pressure-based adaptive ratios in CRITIC.
    *   **Thorough Evaluation:** The framework is rigorously evaluated on benchmark datasets with extensive ablation studies.
    *   **Clear Explanation**: Mathematical formulation and algorithms are presented very clearly, and justified well.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Contributions:** While the integration is novel, the individual components might be considered incremental improvements upon existing techniques.
    *   **Complexity:** The integrated framework is complex and requires careful tuning of several hyperparameters. This could make it difficult for practitioners to adopt.
    *   **Limited Novelty of Individual Components**: Many of the techniques, such as attention gradient analysis and kV caching are very well known. The modifications applied to them are mostly incremental and limited to how they are applied to the architecture
    *   **Evaluation Details:** Some implementation details in the evaluation setting are not clearly stated (what model sizes were used with what datasets, what training budget was used). This makes replication harder.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical and scalable framework for building RAG systems. The techniques presented could be adopted by researchers and practitioners looking to improve the performance of RAG in various applications. The code release (if available) is also an important factor for the community to build upon this work. The modular architecture makes this paper more reusable than prior work.

**Justification for the Score:**

Given the novelty in the *integration* of multiple techniques, the significant challenges addressed, the thorough evaluation, and the potential impact on the field, but also considering the incremental contributions of individual components and the complexity of the framework, a score of **7** is appropriate.

*Rationale:*
- 7: An integration of multiple, good techniques is still useful, though less than any one break-through innovation
- The empirical evaluation appears comprehensive
- The theoretical foundation is more solid than just "hacks" so it's generalizable in usefulness beyond the datasets.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Extracting Formal Specifications from Documents Using LLMs for Automated Testing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01294v1)
- **Authors**: Hui Li, Zhen Dong, Siao Wang, Hui Zhang, Liwei Shen, Xin Peng, Dongdong She
- **Abstract**: Automated testing plays a crucial role in ensuring software security. It heavily relies on formal specifications to validate the correctness of the system behavior. However, the main approach to defining these formal specifications is through manual analysis of software documents, which requires a significant amount of engineering effort from experienced researchers and engineers. Meanwhile, system update further increases the human labor cost to maintain a corresponding formal specification, making the manual analysis approach a time-consuming and error-prone task. Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated promising capabilities in natural language understanding. Yet, the feasibility of using LLMs to automate the extraction of formal specifications from software documents remains unexplored. We conduct an empirical study by constructing a comprehensive dataset comprising 603 specifications from 37 documents across three representative open-source software. We then evaluate the most recent LLMs' capabilities in extracting formal specifications from documents in an end-to-end fashion, including GPT-4o, Claude, and Llama. Our study demonstrates the application of LLMs in formal specification extraction tasks while identifying two major limitations: specification oversimplification and specification fabrication. We attribute these deficiencies to the LLMs' inherent limitations in processing and expressive capabilities, as well as their tendency to fabricate fictional information. Inspired by human cognitive processes, we propose a two-stage method, annotation-then-conversion, to address these challenges. Our method demonstrates significant improvements over the end-to-end method, with a 29.2% increase in the number of correctly extracted specifications and a 14.0% improvement in average accuracy. In particular, our best-performing LLM achieves an accuracy of 71.6%.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the feasibility of using Large Language Models (LLMs) to automate the extraction of formal specifications from software documentation, a process currently reliant on manual effort.  The authors construct a dataset of 603 specifications derived from 37 documents across three open-source software projects: ArduPilot, PX4, and Autoware.  They evaluate the performance of GPT-4o, Claude-3.5-Sonnet, and Llama-3.1-405B in an end-to-end extraction task.  The study reveals limitations of this end-to-end approach, including specification oversimplification and fabrication.  To address these challenges, the authors propose a two-stage "annotation-then-conversion" method, mimicking human cognitive processes.  Experimental results demonstrate that this two-stage approach significantly improves accuracy and reduces false positives compared to the end-to-end approach. Finally the authors replace the second stage by a SOTA method (DeepSTL) from pre-LLM era, the results illustrate that the performance is poorer.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its application of LLMs to the *specific* task of extracting formal specifications from software documents and the proposed two-stage method. Applying LLMs to information extraction is not entirely new, but the focus on generating formal specifications and the analysis of the challenges inherent in this specific task (oversimplification, fabrication) are valuable. The annotation-then-conversion method represents a reasonably novel approach to overcoming these challenges. However, the core idea of decomposing a complex task into simpler stages is a common strategy in AI.

**Significance:** Automating the extraction of formal specifications has the potential to significantly impact software testing and verification. If successful, it could reduce manual effort, improve efficiency, and enhance the reliability of software systems. This paper makes a valuable contribution by identifying the limitations of a naive "end-to-end" LLM approach and proposing a more structured methodology. The empirical evaluation on a realistic dataset adds weight to the findings. The improved method provides a novel approach towards solving the challenges with end-to-end solution.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem being addressed and its importance.
*   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The experimental setup, including dataset construction, LLM selection, and evaluation metrics, is well-defined.
*   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The comparison between the end-to-end and annotation-then-conversion methods is thorough and provides quantitative evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
*   **Insightful Analysis:** The identification of specification oversimplification and fabrication as key challenges is insightful and contributes to a deeper understanding of LLM limitations in this context.
*   **Realistic Dataset:** The use of real-world open-source software projects adds credibility to the study.
*   **Comparison with pre-LLM Approach:** The comparison with DeepSTL highlights the advantages of using LLMs for TL conversion (when properly prompted).

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited LLM Exploration:** The paper only evaluates three LLMs. While they are representative, exploring a wider range of models (especially those with different architectures or training data) could provide a more comprehensive understanding.
*   **Prompt Engineering:** While the paper describes using roleplaying prompt engineering, it is a relative straightforward design using roleplaying prompt. More advanced prompt engineering techniques could be explored to further optimize the LLMs' performance.
*   **Generalizability:** While the chosen software projects are diverse, the findings might not generalize to other types of software documentation or domains.
*   **DeepSTL as Baseline**: The chosen LLM prompting strategy is simple which results in high accuracy, but the baseline DeepSTL is of very limited accuracy and shows only its inferiority. More TL conversion tools should have been compared to emphasize the advances made with LLMs.
*   **Error analysis:** While discussing the issues in TL formulas extracted by DeepSTL, there should have been similar error analysis for the proposed model to improve them.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of automated software testing by exploring the application of LLMs to formal specification extraction. The identification of limitations of end-to-end approaches and the proposition of a two-stage method represent a meaningful advancement. While the research has some limitations, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. Therefore, given the incremental but significant contribution to a challenging and important problem, a score of **7** is appropriate. The paper opens a new avenue for future exploration and contributes new insights in LLM usage, as the paper provides a more effective technique over end-to-end process with LLM, also more effectively than previous SOTA tool, however with ample room for improvements which are explained in detail.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### ThinkPrune: Pruning Long Chain-of-Thought of LLMs via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01296v1)
- **Authors**: Bairu Hou, Yang Zhang, Jiabao Ji, Yujian Liu, Kaizhi Qian, Jacob Andreas, Shiyu Chang
- **Abstract**: We present ThinkPrune, a simple yet effective method for pruning the thinking length for long-thinking LLMs, which has been found to often produce inefficient and redundant thinking processes. Existing preliminary explorations of reducing thinking length primarily focus on forcing the thinking process to early exit, rather than adapting the LLM to optimize and consolidate the thinking process, and therefore the length-performance tradeoff observed so far is sub-optimal. To fill this gap, ThinkPrune offers a simple solution that continuously trains the long-thinking LLMs via reinforcement learning (RL) with an added token limit, beyond which any unfinished thoughts and answers will be discarded, resulting in a zero reward. To further preserve model performance, we introduce an iterative length pruning approach, where multiple rounds of RL are conducted, each with an increasingly more stringent token limit. We observed that ThinkPrune results in a remarkable performance-length tradeoff -- on the AIME24 dataset, the reasoning length of DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B can be reduced by half with only 2% drop in performance. We also observed that after pruning, the LLMs can bypass unnecessary steps while keeping the core reasoning process complete. Code is available at https://github.com/UCSB-NLP-Chang/ThinkPrune.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "THINKPRUNE: Pruning Long Chain-of-Thought of LLMs via Reinforcement Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces THINKPRUNE, a method for reducing the token length of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs) by using Reinforcement Learning (RL).  The core idea is to train LLMs with a token limit; models exceeding this limit receive a zero reward. To mitigate performance degradation, the method employs an iterative pruning strategy where the token limit is gradually reduced over multiple rounds of RL training. The paper demonstrates that THINKPRUNE can substantially reduce the length of reasoning chains while preserving, or even slightly improving, performance on mathematical reasoning benchmarks. Analysis reveals that the method helps LLMs to avoid unnecessary reasoning steps, remain focused on the question, and improve inference efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The idea of using RL to control CoT length isn't entirely new; prior work has explored length penalties. The novelty lies in the **iterative pruning approach combined with a hard token limit during training**.  This strategy differentiates it from methods that simply truncate outputs at inference time (budget forcing), as it aims to **train the model to reason more efficiently** from the outset. The insights into how reasoning patterns change after pruning is also a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** Long CoT chains can lead to computational overhead. A method like THINKPRUNE, if generally applicable, can improve the practical efficiency of LLMs, making them more accessible for resource-constrained environments. The paper demonstrates empirical gains in reasoning length reduction with minimal performance impact on several models and datasets, indicating the potential for widespread adoption. However, the benchmarks used are primarily mathematical reasoning, so generalizability needs further investigation.  The method’s ability to remove redundant self-checking steps while preserving core reasoning is also a significant finding.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Simplicity:** The method is relatively simple to implement and doesn't require extensive reward engineering or hyperparameter tuning beyond standard RL fine-tuning.
    *   **Empirical Results:**  The paper provides compelling empirical evidence of the effectiveness of THINKPRUNE, including performance on multiple benchmarks and a clear analysis of the trade-off between reasoning length and accuracy.
    *   **Iterative Approach:**  The iterative pruning strategy is a smart way to mitigate performance degradation that can occur when simply imposing a hard token limit.
    *   **Analysis:** The analysis of the changes in reasoning behavior (keyword frequencies, reasoning step distributions, perplexity) provides insights into *how* THINKPRUNE works and reinforces the claim that it's not just sacrificing performance for length reduction.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The evaluation primarily focuses on mathematical reasoning.  It is unclear how THINKPRUNE would perform on other reasoning tasks like commonsense reasoning or logical inference.
    *   **Sensitivity to Hyperparameters:** Although presented as simple, the success of the iterative pruning likely depends on carefully choosing the initial length limit and the rate at which the limit is reduced.  The paper could benefit from more details on how these hyperparameters were determined.
    *   **Potential Negative Impacts on Creativity or Exploration:** By limiting reasoning length, there's a risk of stifling creativity or preventing the model from exploring novel or unconventional solutions. This is not explicitly addressed.
    *   **Limited Applicability to Certain Model Architectures:** The method assumes that the model can be fine-tuned using RL. Some models have restrictions that prevent fine-tuning.

*   **Potential Influence:** THINKPRUNE offers a practical approach to addressing the "overthinking" problem in LLMs, with a minimal negative impact. This method will likely influence future research aimed at improving the efficiency and cost-effectiveness of LLMs. The iterative pruning approach could also be adapted for other forms of model compression or simplification.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is well-written, technically sound, and presents a method that is both novel and potentially impactful. While the evaluation is somewhat limited in scope, the positive results, clear analysis, and simplicity of the approach justify a relatively high score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Bi-LAT: Bilateral Control-Based Imitation Learning via Natural Language and Action Chunking with Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01301v1)
- **Authors**: Takumi Kobayashi, Masato Kobayashi, Thanpimon Buamanee, Yuki Uranishi
- **Abstract**: We present Bi-LAT, a novel imitation learning framework that unifies bilateral control with natural language processing to achieve precise force modulation in robotic manipulation. Bi-LAT leverages joint position, velocity, and torque data from leader-follower teleoperation while also integrating visual and linguistic cues to dynamically adjust applied force. By encoding human instructions such as "softly grasp the cup" or "strongly twist the sponge" through a multimodal Transformer-based model, Bi-LAT learns to distinguish nuanced force requirements in real-world tasks. We demonstrate Bi-LAT's performance in (1) unimanual cup-stacking scenario where the robot accurately modulates grasp force based on language commands, and (2) bimanual sponge-twisting task that requires coordinated force control. Experimental results show that Bi-LAT effectively reproduces the instructed force levels, particularly when incorporating SigLIP among tested language encoders. Our findings demonstrate the potential of integrating natural language cues into imitation learning, paving the way for more intuitive and adaptive human-robot interaction. For additional material, please visit: https://mertcookimg.github.io/bi-lat/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Bi-LAT, a novel imitation learning framework that combines bilateral control with natural language processing to achieve precise force modulation in robotic manipulation. Bi-LAT uses joint position, velocity, and torque data from leader-follower teleoperation, alongside visual and linguistic cues, to dynamically adjust applied force.  The system employs a multimodal Transformer-based model to learn force requirements from language commands like "softly grasp the cup" or "strongly twist the sponge." The authors validate Bi-LAT through two real-world tasks: unimanual cup-stacking and bimanual sponge-twisting. The experiments demonstrate Bi-LAT's ability to reproduce instructed force levels effectively, particularly when integrating SigLIP as the language encoder, suggesting the potential of incorporating natural language cues for more intuitive and adaptive human-robot interaction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty of the paper lies in its integration of several existing techniques (bilateral control, imitation learning, transformers, language encoders) into a single framework *specifically* designed to connect natural language instructions to precise force modulation in robotic manipulation. While each individual component is well-established, the particular combination and the focus on force control guided by language make this contribution more novel than a simple aggregation of methods. It extends prior bilateral control-based imitation learning by adding language-based control over the *degree* of force applied. The comparison to Bi-ACT is crucial here. Bi-LAT is clearly an evolution of Bi-ACT, but the addition of language and the focus on force modulation driven by that language constitutes a significant step forward.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a key limitation in existing robotic manipulation systems, namely the difficulty of translating human intentions (expressed in natural language) into precise force control. The ability to instruct a robot to "softly" grasp or "strongly" twist has significant implications for safe and effective interaction with fragile or deformable objects. This has relevance for applications like assistive robotics, manufacturing, and even surgery.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The experimental validation is well-designed, demonstrating the effectiveness of Bi-LAT in both unimanual and bimanual tasks. The comparison between different language encoders (DistilBERT, ModernBERT, CLIP, SigLIP) provides valuable insights into their performance in this specific context. The quantitative data (success rates, joint angle/torque distributions) supports the claims made in the paper.
    *   The paper is clearly written and well-organized, making it easy to understand the proposed framework and the experimental results.
    *   The authors address limitations of earlier unilateral setups, and provide a detailed explanation of how bilateral setups allow for a more nuanced integration of force modulation.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experiments, while demonstrating the framework's capabilities, are relatively simple. Future work should address more complex and dynamic scenarios.
    *   The reliance on teleoperation for data collection might limit the scalability of the approach. Exploring methods for generating synthetic data or using reinforcement learning to refine the learned policies could be beneficial.
    *   The discussion of prompt engineering, while mentioned, lacks depth. A more thorough analysis of the impact of different prompting strategies would strengthen the paper.
    *   The failure case in the bimanual experiment (one failure out of five in the "Strongly twist" condition) requires more detailed explanation. What specific limitations of the model or the environment led to this failure?

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in imitation learning, robotic manipulation, and human-robot interaction. It highlights the importance of incorporating language cues for fine-grained control and provides a solid foundation for building more intuitive and adaptive robotic systems.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper builds upon existing techniques, the specific combination aimed at language-driven force modulation is novel and significant. The clear experimental validation and the potential for impact in various applications justify a relatively high score. However, the simplicity of the experiments and the limitations related to data collection and generalization prevent it from being an exceptional contribution. A more in-depth analysis of failure cases and future scalability would have significantly boosted the score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Real-time Ad retrieval via LLM-generative Commercial Intention for Sponsored Search Advertising
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01304v1)
- **Authors**: Tongtong Liu, Zhaohui Wang, Meiyue Qin, Zenghui Lu, Xudong Chen, Yuekui Yang, Peng Shu
- **Abstract**: The integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) with retrieval systems has shown promising potential in retrieving documents (docs) or advertisements (ads) for a given query. Existing LLM-based retrieval methods generate numeric or content-based DocIDs to retrieve docs/ads. However, the one-to-few mapping between numeric IDs and docs, along with the time-consuming content extraction, leads to semantic inefficiency and limits scalability in large-scale corpora. In this paper, we propose the Real-time Ad REtrieval (RARE) framework, which leverages LLM-generated text called Commercial Intentions (CIs) as an intermediate semantic representation to directly retrieve ads for queries in real-time. These CIs are generated by a customized LLM injected with commercial knowledge, enhancing its domain relevance. Each CI corresponds to multiple ads, yielding a lightweight and scalable set of CIs. RARE has been implemented in a real-world online system, handling daily search volumes in the hundreds of millions. The online implementation has yielded significant benefits: a 5.04% increase in consumption, a 6.37% rise in Gross Merchandise Volume (GMV), a 1.28% enhancement in click-through rate (CTR) and a 5.29% increase in shallow conversions. Extensive offline experiments show RARE's superiority over ten competitive baselines in four major categories.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RARE, a novel real-time ad retrieval framework that uses Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate Commercial Intentions (CIs). CIs serve as an intermediate semantic representation to connect user queries directly to relevant advertisements. Unlike traditional methods reliant on keywords or document IDs (DocIDs), RARE leverages a customized LLM, injected with commercial knowledge, to generate these CIs. This approach addresses limitations in semantic understanding and scalability associated with other LLM-based retrieval methods. RARE has been deployed in a real-world online advertising system and shows significant performance improvements in consumption, Gross Merchandise Value (GMV), click-through rate (CTR), and conversions compared to several baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a significant problem in real-time ad retrieval: bridging the semantic gap between user queries and advertisements while maintaining scalability. RARE offers a practical solution by introducing Commercial Intentions (CIs), which effectively acts as a compressed semantic representation.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** The idea of using LLM-generated CIs as intermediate semantic representations for ad retrieval is novel and demonstrates a good understanding of the limitations of existing keyword-based and DocID-based systems.
*   **Practical Implementation:** The paper details the implementation of RARE in a real-world online system, processing high query volumes, thereby showcasing its feasibility and scalability. This is a significant strength compared to purely theoretical papers.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The online A/B testing results are compelling, showing significant improvements in key advertising metrics. The offline experiments provide a solid comparative analysis with various baselines across different categories. The ablation study further strengthens the results by isolating the impact of each component.
*   **Knowledge Injection & Format Fine-Tuning:** The custom LLM fine-tuning strategies are appropriate and are key to the system.
*   **Efficiency:** Caching strategy and beam search optimization further strengthen the system and make it usable for millions of users.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Technical Depth:** While the paper describes the overall architecture and process, it lacks detailed technical specifications, particularly regarding the LLM customization (knowledge injection and fine-tuning). More specifics on the data used, fine-tuning loss functions, and implementation details of constrained beam search and caching would enhance the reproducibility and understanding of the method. Details on the implementation of specialized GPU cluster with hundred of L40s would further strengthen the work.
*   **Lack of a More Comprehensive Ablation:** The ablation study removes key components in the framework, but does not perform finer-grained ablations on elements such as the size of the commercial knowledge base or the specific hyperparameters used for training.
*   **Baselines:** the retrieval baselines can be improved and can include more recent, modern LLM-based ad retrieval techniques.
*   **Generalizability of CIs:** The paper does not deeply explore the generalizability of the generated CIs to different advertising domains or languages.
*   **Lack of Error Analysis:** A detailed error analysis, showing specific examples of how RARE overcomes limitations of other systems and analyzing the types of queries/ads where it still struggles, would strengthen the paper.

**Significance:**

The paper is significant as it showcases a practical application of LLMs in real-time ad retrieval, an area that demands both semantic understanding and scalability. RARE's performance improvements in a real-world system demonstrate its potential impact on the advertising industry. The introduction of CIs provides a new way to leverage LLMs for semantic retrieval.

**Overall:**

RARE presents a novel approach to ad retrieval that offers practical benefits. It leverages LLMs in a way that addresses the challenges of scalability and semantic understanding. The paper has some weaknesses in technical depth and evaluation, but the overall impact of RARE as a functional system with demonstrated improvements is compelling.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** RARE shows a practical, working system that overcomes some scalability and semantic limitations of previous systems. It introduces the new commercial intent (CI) concept, and proves that it works well.

I am penalizing the work for lack of detailed technical specifics. The work shows novel results. The offline experiments demonstrate superiority to existing solutions.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Safeguarding Vision-Language Models: Mitigating Vulnerabilities to Gaussian Noise in Perturbation-based Attacks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01308v1)
- **Authors**: Jiawei Wang, Yushen Zuo, Yuanjun Chai, Zhendong Liu, Yichen Fu, Yichun Feng, Kin-man Lam
- **Abstract**: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) extend the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) by incorporating visual information, yet they remain vulnerable to jailbreak attacks, especially when processing noisy or corrupted images. Although existing VLMs adopt security measures during training to mitigate such attacks, vulnerabilities associated with noise-augmented visual inputs are overlooked. In this work, we identify that missing noise-augmented training causes critical security gaps: many VLMs are susceptible to even simple perturbations such as Gaussian noise. To address this challenge, we propose Robust-VLGuard, a multimodal safety dataset with aligned / misaligned image-text pairs, combined with noise-augmented fine-tuning that reduces attack success rates while preserving functionality of VLM. For stronger optimization-based visual perturbation attacks, we propose DiffPure-VLM, leveraging diffusion models to convert adversarial perturbations into Gaussian-like noise, which can be defended by VLMs with noise-augmented safety fine-tuning. Experimental results demonstrate that the distribution-shifting property of diffusion model aligns well with our fine-tuned VLMs, significantly mitigating adversarial perturbations across varying intensities. The dataset and code are available at https://github.com/JarvisUSTC/DiffPure-RobustVLM.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Safeguarding Vision-Language Models: Mitigating Vulnerabilities to Gaussian Noise in Perturbation-based Attacks" investigates the susceptibility of Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to adversarial attacks involving even minor perturbations, such as Gaussian noise. It identifies a lack of robustness to such noise in existing VLMs, proposes the "Robust-VLGuard" dataset with aligned/misaligned image-text pairs for noise-augmented fine-tuning, and introduces "DiffPure-VLM," a defense pipeline leveraging diffusion models to transform adversarial perturbations into Gaussian-like noise, which the fine-tuned VLMs are better equipped to handle. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of its approach in mitigating adversarial attacks across various intensities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in several aspects. First, it systematically identifies and highlights a previously under-emphasized vulnerability of VLMs to even simple Gaussian noise, disrupting both helpfulness and safety alignment. Second, the Robust-VLGuard dataset introduces a novel concept of image-text *misalignment* within safety-focused fine-tuning, addressing a critical scenario often overlooked by other safety datasets. Third, the combination of noise-augmented fine-tuning with a diffusion-based pre-processing step (DiffPure-VLM) presents a unique defense strategy that leverages the distribution-shifting capabilities of diffusion models, transforming adversarial noise into a form the VLM can more readily handle. While diffusion-based defenses have been explored previously in other contexts, their adaptation and integration specifically for improving the robustness of fine-tuned VLMs against diverse visual adversarial attacks is a significant contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is multi-faceted. VLMs are becoming increasingly prevalent, so their vulnerabilities need to be addressed.  The findings regarding the lack of inherent robustness to Gaussian noise are important because they show that even basic noise can disrupt the functionality of advanced models.  The Robust-VLGuard dataset provides a valuable resource for the community to develop more robust and safe VLMs. DiffPure-VLM offers a practical defense mechanism against a broad range of adversarial attacks, which is essential for the reliable deployment of VLMs. The results demonstrate the practical effectiveness of the proposed methods, substantially improving VLM robustness with minimal impact on baseline performance. By tackling the problem of noise vulnerability early in the VLM development cycle, this work paves the way for more secure and reliable multimodal systems.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and systematic analysis of VLM vulnerabilities to noise.
    *   Novel dataset incorporating image-text misalignment for improved safety fine-tuning.
    *   Effective defense pipeline (DiffPure-VLM) leveraging diffusion models.
    *   Comprehensive experiments demonstrating improved robustness and generalization.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The computational cost associated with DiffPure, while potentially reduced with optimized diffusion model implementations, could be a barrier to broader adoption. The trade-off between defense efficacy and computational efficiency requires further exploration, particularly with respect to practical deployment scenarios.
    *   The study primarily focuses on specific types of adversarial attacks (Gaussian noise and PGD). More comprehensive evaluation against a wider spectrum of attack methodologies is needed to establish the robustness of DiffPure-VLM across different adversarial landscapes.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth theoretical analysis of the impact of image-text misalignment on VLM safety and helpfulness. While the experimental results are compelling, further insights into the underlying mechanisms would strengthen the overall contribution.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by raising awareness of noise-related vulnerabilities in VLMs and by providing practical tools and strategies for mitigating these vulnerabilities.
