The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of MLLM security. While there are some limitations, the novelty of the approach, the convincing experimental results, and the responsible disclosure of the findings make it a significant contribution. The paper effectively highlights the unique vulnerabilities introduced by multi-modal integration and code contextualization.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper demonstrates good novelty and significance in identifying and exploiting vulnerabilities in MLLMs. However, limitations in the dataset size, the lack of in-depth analysis of model-specific vulnerabilities, a reliance on GPT-4 for metric scoring, and the relatively shallow defense analysis prevent it from achieving a higher score. It has clear influence on the need for cross-modal security research.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Enabling Systematic Generalization in Abstract Spatial Reasoning through Meta-Learning for Compositionality
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01445v1)
- **Authors**: Philipp Mondorf, Shijia Zhou, Monica Riedler, Barbara Plank
- **Abstract**: Systematic generalization refers to the capacity to understand and generate novel combinations from known components. Despite recent progress by large language models (LLMs) across various domains, these models often fail to extend their knowledge to novel compositional scenarios, revealing notable limitations in systematic generalization. There has been an ongoing debate about whether neural networks possess the capacity for systematic generalization, with recent studies suggesting that meta-learning approaches designed for compositionality can significantly enhance this ability. However, these insights have largely been confined to linguistic problems, leaving their applicability to other tasks an open question. In this study, we extend the approach of meta-learning for compositionality to the domain of abstract spatial reasoning. To this end, we introduce $\textit{SYGAR}$-a dataset designed to evaluate the capacity of models to systematically generalize from known geometric transformations (e.g., translation, rotation) of two-dimensional objects to novel combinations of these transformations (e.g., translation+rotation). Our results show that a transformer-based encoder-decoder model, trained via meta-learning for compositionality, can systematically generalize to previously unseen transformation compositions, significantly outperforming state-of-the-art LLMs, including o3-mini, GPT-4o, and Gemini 2.0 Flash, which fail to exhibit similar systematic behavior. Our findings highlight the effectiveness of meta-learning in promoting systematicity beyond linguistic tasks, suggesting a promising direction toward more robust and generalizable models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SYGAR, a new dataset designed to evaluate systematic generalization in abstract spatial reasoning. It focuses on the ability of models to combine known geometric transformations (translation, rotation, reflection, extension, and color change) into novel compositions. The authors train a transformer-based encoder-decoder model using meta-learning for compositionality (MLC) and demonstrate that it significantly outperforms state-of-the-art Large Language Models (LLMs) like GPT-40, o3-mini, and Gemini 2.0 Flash on this task. The paper highlights the potential of MLC for promoting systematicity beyond linguistic tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in applying meta-learning for compositionality to the *spatial reasoning* domain, building upon previous work primarily focused on linguistic tasks.  Introducing the SYGAR dataset is also a contribution, as it provides a controlled environment to evaluate systematic generalization with geometric transformations. However, the core idea of using meta-learning for compositionality is not entirely new, having been established by Lake & Baroni (2023) in the context of pseudolanguage instruction learning.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is in showing that meta-learning can enable neural networks to systematically generalize in non-linguistic domains. The demonstrated failure of powerful LLMs to perform well on this task underscores the ongoing challenges in achieving true systematicity. The paper provides empirical evidence that the MLC approach is effective in abstract spatial reasoning, suggesting a promising avenue for developing more robust and generalizable AI models. By showing systematic generalization in spatial reasoning, it extends the application of meta-learning to a domain more closely tied to physical and robotic applications than previous works.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and well-designed dataset (SYGAR).
    *   Strong empirical results demonstrating the superiority of the MLC approach over LLMs.
    *   Ablation studies providing insights into the importance of different components of the MLC training procedure.
    *   Reproducibility statement with publicly available code to support further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The core idea of using meta-learning for compositionality is not entirely novel.
    *   The scope of geometric transformations is relatively limited (e.g., only 90-degree rotations, one-cell translations). It might not fully capture the complexity of spatial reasoning in real-world scenarios.
    *   Although demonstrating a clear advantage of the MLC model, it is not entirely clear *why* the LLMs fail as dramatically as they do. What are their specific limitations in spatial abstraction?
    *   Lack of direct comparison to non-meta-learning-based models fine-tuned specifically on the SYGAR dataset.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in areas such as:
    *   Systematic generalization in AI.
    *   Meta-learning and few-shot learning.
    *   Spatial reasoning and abstract reasoning.
    *   Robot learning and artificial general intelligence (AGI).

**Justification:**

The paper provides solid evidence that MLC can improve systematic generalization in a new task domain (abstract spatial reasoning).  It builds upon existing work in a meaningful way and provides valuable empirical results. However, the relative novelty of applying meta-learning to compositionality is somewhat limited, which impacts the overall score. The analysis of LLMs is somewhat weak, focusing more on highlighting their failures than explaining *why* the specific architecture fails to solve the tasks at hand, and there is no comparison to supervised models trained specifically on the SYGAR task. While the experiments are well-executed, the conclusions do not make substantial shifts in the field.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### GeoRAG: A Question-Answering Approach from a Geographical Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01458v1)
- **Authors**: Jian Wang, Zhuo Zhao, Zheng Jie Wang, Bo Da Cheng, Lei Nie, Wen Luo, Zhao Yuan Yu, Ling Wang Yuan
- **Abstract**: Geographic Question Answering (GeoQA) addresses natural language queries in geographic domains to fulfill complex user demands and improve information retrieval efficiency. Traditional QA systems, however, suffer from limited comprehension, low retrieval accuracy, weak interactivity, and inadequate handling of complex tasks, hindering precise information acquisition. This study presents GeoRAG, a knowledge-enhanced QA framework integrating domain-specific fine-tuning and prompt engineering with Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) technology to enhance geographic knowledge retrieval accuracy and user interaction. The methodology involves four components: (1) A structured geographic knowledge base constructed from 3267 corpora (research papers, monographs, and technical reports), categorized via a multi-agent approach into seven dimensions: semantic understanding, spatial location, geometric morphology, attribute characteristics, feature relationships, evolutionary processes, and operational mechanisms. This yielded 145234 classified entries and 875432 multi-dimensional QA pairs. (2) A multi-label text classifier based on BERT-Base-Chinese, trained to analyze query types through geographic dimension classification. (3) A retrieval evaluator leveraging QA pair data to assess query-document relevance, optimizing retrieval precision. (4) GeoPrompt templates engineered to dynamically integrate user queries with retrieved information, enhancing response quality through dimension-specific prompting. Comparative experiments demonstrate GeoRAG's superior performance over conventional RAG across multiple base models, validating its generalizability. This work advances geographic AI by proposing a novel paradigm for deploying large language models in domain-specific contexts, with implications for improving GeoQA systems scalability and accuracy in real-world applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GeoRAG, a novel geographical question answering (GeoQA) approach that enhances Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) by incorporating domain fine-tuning, prompt engineering, and geographical perspectives. GeoRAG integrates a structured geographical knowledge base (constructed from research papers, monographs, and technical reports categorized into seven geographical dimensions), a multi-label text classifier to determine query types, a retrieval evaluator using QA pairs, and GeoPrompt templates. The authors demonstrate GeoRAG's superior performance and generalizability compared to conventional RAG implementations, positioning it as an advancement in GeoAI technologies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper exhibits moderate novelty. While RAG is a well-established technique, its application and optimization for the geographical domain with a structured knowledge base and dimension-specific treatment represent a valuable contribution. The seven-dimensional taxonomy for classifying geographical questions is also a unique aspect of this work. However, many components build upon existing techniques like BERT and multi-agent systems.
*   **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing a specific need within the GeoQA domain, where general-purpose LLMs struggle with complex, context-dependent geographical knowledge. By improving retrieval precision and answer accuracy, GeoRAG potentially enhances applications in education, research, and policy formulation. The study provides a solid foundation for further GeoAI advancements.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing QA approaches in the geographical domain.
    *   **Structured Knowledge Base:** The creation of a structured knowledge base and the categorization of geographical questions are crucial for improving retrieval accuracy.
    *   **Dimension-Specific Approach:** The use of dimension-specific retrieval strategies, evaluators, and prompts enhances the relevance and quality of the generated answers.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The comparative experiments with various base models and evaluation metrics demonstrate the effectiveness of GeoRAG.
    *   **Multi-agent system for data creation:** Novel and efficient data generation technique.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The GeoRAG framework is complex, involving multiple components and fine-tuning processes. The implementation may require significant resources and expertise.
    *   **Dependency on Domain Knowledge:**  The approach heavily relies on the quality and completeness of the geographical knowledge base.
    *   **Lack of direct comparisons:** Although comparing against base RAG and LLMs the comparison would be stronger if it included other SOTA domain specific Q&A techniques for GeoQA.
    *   **Evaluation may be biased:** Data was generated by an LLM. Since GeoRAG uses an LLM in the pipeline evaluation accuracy is probably biased.

*   **Potential Influence:** GeoRAG can influence future research in GeoAI by providing a framework for domain-specific RAG optimization. It can also inspire the development of similar approaches for other vertical domains requiring specialized knowledge retrieval.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to GeoQA by adapting and optimizing RAG for the geographical domain. The structured knowledge base and dimension-specific approach enhance retrieval precision and answer accuracy. The experimental results demonstrate GeoRAG's superior performance and generalizability. However, the complexity of the framework, heavy reliance on domain knowledge and potential for biased evaluation limits its overall impact. The potential for practical application and its influence on future research in GeoAI justifies a score of 7.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### ANNEXE: Unified Analyzing, Answering, and Pixel Grounding for Egocentric Interaction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01472v1)
- **Authors**: Yuejiao Su, Yi Wang, Qiongyang Hu, Chuang Yang, Lap-Pui Chau
- **Abstract**: Egocentric interaction perception is one of the essential branches in investigating human-environment interaction, which lays the basis for developing next-generation intelligent systems. However, existing egocentric interaction understanding methods cannot yield coherent textual and pixel-level responses simultaneously according to user queries, which lacks flexibility for varying downstream application requirements. To comprehend egocentric interactions exhaustively, this paper presents a novel task named Egocentric Interaction Reasoning and pixel Grounding (Ego-IRG). Taking an egocentric image with the query as input, Ego-IRG is the first task that aims to resolve the interactions through three crucial steps: analyzing, answering, and pixel grounding, which results in fluent textual and fine-grained pixel-level responses. Another challenge is that existing datasets cannot meet the conditions for the Ego-IRG task. To address this limitation, this paper creates the Ego-IRGBench dataset based on extensive manual efforts, which includes over 20k egocentric images with 1.6 million queries and corresponding multimodal responses about interactions. Moreover, we design a unified ANNEXE model to generate text- and pixel-level outputs utilizing multimodal large language models, which enables a comprehensive interpretation of egocentric interactions. The experiments on the Ego-IRGBench exhibit the effectiveness of our ANNEXE model compared with other works.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces a novel task called "Egocentric Interaction Reasoning and pixel Grounding" (Ego-IRG), which aims to provide a more comprehensive understanding of human-environment interactions from a first-person perspective.  The Ego-IRG task involves analyzing, answering, and pixel grounding based on user queries related to egocentric images.  To facilitate research on this task, the authors created a large-scale dataset called Ego-IRGBench, built upon the HOI4D dataset, with extensive manual annotation of interaction descriptions, query-answer pairs, and pixel-level grounding. The paper also proposes a unified depth-assisted neural network architecture named ANNEXE, which leverages multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to generate both text-level and pixel-level responses. Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the ANNEXE model compared to other existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has several novel aspects:

    *   The definition of the Ego-IRG task is a significant contribution. It tackles a gap in existing research by unifying analyzing, answering, and pixel grounding for egocentric interactions.
    *   The creation of the Ego-IRGBench dataset is valuable.  Existing datasets lack the necessary annotations for the Ego-IRG task, making this a crucial resource. The effort involved in manually annotating over 20k images with rich descriptions and query-response-mask pairs is substantial.
    *   The ANNEXE model is a novel attempt to generate both text- and pixel-level outputs simultaneously using MLLMs. While MLLMs have been used in similar contexts, this is the first end-to-end model for Ego-IRG.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of egocentric vision and human-computer interaction in several ways:

    *   By introducing the Ego-IRG task, the paper provides a clear benchmark for evaluating models that aim for a comprehensive understanding of egocentric interactions.
    *   The Ego-IRGBench dataset can serve as a valuable resource for training and evaluating models for Ego-IRG and related tasks.
    *   The ANNEXE model demonstrates the feasibility of using MLLMs to generate coherent text- and pixel-level responses, which can inspire further research in this direction.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clearly defines the Ego-IRG task and provides strong motivation for its importance.
    *   Presents a comprehensive and well-annotated dataset (Ego-IRGBench).
    *   Proposes a novel architecture (ANNEXE) that leverages MLLMs effectively.
    *   Provides detailed experimental results that demonstrate the effectiveness of the ANNEXE model.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The method builds on existing MLLMs, but the specific architecture of the mask generator is relatively simple. Further exploration of more sophisticated mask generation techniques could improve performance.
    *   The paper does not deeply analyze the limitations of current MLLMs for this specific task or suggest directions for future research that could overcome those limitations.
    *   The ablation studies could be more extensive. For example, ablating different components of the ANNEXE architecture (beyond the depth estimation) could provide more insight into the contribution of each component.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper provides a novel task formulation (Ego-IRG), a valuable dataset (Ego-IRGBench), and a working model (ANNEXE) that combines these components. It's a solid contribution, but the ANNEXE model, while effective, isn't a radically new architectural concept in itself. The reliance on an existing MLLM as a backbone reduces the overall architectural novelty. Therefore, while the paper introduces a significant new problem and dataset, the solution is more of an engineering effort that combines existing technologies, albeit effectively.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Are Autonomous Web Agents Good Testers?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01495v1)
- **Authors**: Antoine Chevrot, Alexandre Vernotte, Jean-Rémy Falleri, Xavier Blanc, Bruno Legeard
- **Abstract**: Despite advances in automated testing, manual testing remains prevalent due to the high maintenance demands associated with test script fragility-scripts often break with minor changes in application structure. Recent developments in Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a potential alternative by powering Autonomous Web Agents (AWAs) that can autonomously interact with applications. These agents may serve as Autonomous Test Agents (ATAs), potentially reducing the need for maintenance-heavy automated scripts by utilising natural language instructions similar to those used by human testers. This paper investigates the feasibility of adapting AWAs for natural language test case execution and how to evaluate them. We contribute with (1) a benchmark of three offline web applications, and a suite of 113 manual test cases, split between passing and failing cases, to evaluate and compare ATAs performance, (2) SeeAct-ATA and pinATA, two open-source ATA implementations capable of executing test steps, verifying assertions and giving verdicts, and (3) comparative experiments using our benchmark that quantifies our ATAs effectiveness. Finally we also proceed to a qualitative evaluation to identify the limitations of PinATA, our best performing implementation. Our findings reveal that our simple implementation, SeeAct-ATA, does not perform well compared to our more advanced PinATA implementation when executing test cases (50% performance improvement). However, while PinATA obtains around 60% of correct verdict and up to a promising 94% specificity, we identify several limitations that need to be addressed to develop more resilient and reliable ATAs, paving the way for robust, low maintenance test automation. CCS Concepts: $\bullet$ Software and its engineering $\rightarrow$ Software testing and debugging.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

This paper investigates the feasibility of using Autonomous Web Agents (AWAs), powered by Large Language Models (LLMs), as Autonomous Test Agents (ATAs) for web application testing. The authors create a benchmark consisting of three offline web applications and 113 manual test cases. They implement two open-source ATAs, SeeAct-ATA and PinATA, and compare their performance on the benchmark. The results show that PinATA outperforms SeeAct-ATA, but both ATAs exhibit limitations, particularly in assertion verifiability and test case conformity. The paper concludes by identifying key challenges and potential areas for improvement in autonomous test execution.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper explores a timely and relevant topic, given the advancements in LLMs and their potential to automate tasks. The application of AWAs to web application testing as ATAs is a logical step, but the paper's main novelty lies in the implementation and evaluation of two specific ATAs and the creation of a benchmark. While some commercial ATA products exist, the paper's transparent, open-source approach and focus on dissecting the challenges is valuable. The proposed metrics for assessing ATA performance are a meaningful extension beyond what's typically used for assessing plain AWAs.

*   **Significance:** The paper contributes to the field by providing a concrete implementation and evaluation of ATAs. The benchmark is a valuable resource that can be used by other researchers to compare different ATA architectures. The identification of limitations, such as challenges in assertion verifiability and test case conformity, is crucial for guiding future research in this area. The paper also fosters a clearer understanding of the strengths and limitations of different approaches to building ATAs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Open-source implementation:** The open-source implementation of SeeAct-ATA and PinATA allows other researchers to reproduce and extend the work.
    *   **Realistic benchmark:** The benchmark consists of realistic web applications and test cases, making the evaluation more relevant to real-world scenarios.
    *   **Detailed analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the performance of the ATAs, identifying specific limitations and challenges.
    *   **Comprehensive metrics:** The use of comprehensive metrics tailored to ATA performance helps in a robust evaluation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited scope:** The evaluation is limited to three specific web applications and a relatively small set of test cases. The generalizability of the results to other types of web applications and test cases is not fully established.
    *   **Simple ATAs:** While the open-source nature of SeeAct-ATA and PinATA is beneficial, their relative simplicity might not fully capture the capabilities of more advanced, potentially proprietary, ATA solutions.
    *   **Some limited discussion of related work:** While the paper does touch upon related work, there could be a more comprehensive discussion and comparison with existing test automation frameworks and tools. The focus is very narrow on LLM applications.
    *   **Reproducibility concerns:** The paper mentions GPT-4’s stochasticity and therefore slight differences between executions remain possible. This can complicate the reproducibility of experiments, despite the set temperature of 0.
    *   **Type III Errors:** The paper notes that a correct verdict doesn’t mean the agent arrived at it correctly, and implements new metrics. Still, it acknowledges that these metrics are primarily for failed tests and passing tests may still be incorrect. Further mitigation would benefit construct validity.

*   **Potential influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a foundation for future research in autonomous test execution. The benchmark and open-source implementations can be used by other researchers to compare different ATA architectures and address the identified limitations. The findings of the paper can also inform the development of more robust and reliable ATAs. The effort can help to shift testing from a manual practice to a more automated one, potentially improving software quality while reducing development costs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to a relevant area by providing a tangible implementation of ATAs and a benchmark for evaluating their performance. It also transparently reports the limitations encountered. However, the limited scope of the evaluation and the relative simplicity of the ATAs prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work. The paper falls short of having a major transformative impact, but the work is well-executed, thoughtfully analyzed, and provides a valuable resource for the community.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Chain of Correction for Full-text Speech Recognition with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01519v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiyuan Tang, Dong Wang, Zhikai Zhou, Yong Liu, Shen Huang, Shidong Shang
- **Abstract**: Full-text error correction with Large Language Models (LLMs) for Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) has gained increased attention due to its potential to correct errors across long contexts and address a broader spectrum of error types, including punctuation restoration and inverse text normalization. Nevertheless, many challenges persist, including issues related to stability, controllability, completeness, and fluency. To mitigate these challenges, this paper proposes the Chain of Correction (CoC) for full-text error correction with LLMs, which corrects errors segment by segment using pre-recognized text as guidance within a regular multi-turn chat format. The CoC also uses pre-recognized full text for context, allowing the model to better grasp global semantics and maintain a comprehensive overview of the entire content. Utilizing the open-sourced full-text error correction dataset ChFT, we fine-tune a pre-trained LLM to evaluate the performance of the CoC framework. Experimental results demonstrate that the CoC effectively corrects errors in full-text ASR outputs, significantly outperforming baseline and benchmark systems. We further analyze how to set the correction threshold to balance under-correction and over-rephrasing, extrapolate the CoC model on extremely long ASR outputs, and investigate whether other types of information can be employed to guide the error correction process.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Chain of Correction (CoC), a novel framework for full-text error correction in Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) using Large Language Models (LLMs).  CoC tackles limitations of previous LLM-based error correction methods by correcting errors segment-by-segment, using pre-recognized text as guidance in a multi-turn chat format. This approach leverages the global context of the full text and allows for better control, stability, completeness, and fluency in error correction. Experiments on the Chinese Full-text Error Correction Dataset (ChFT) demonstrate that CoC significantly outperforms baseline and benchmark systems. The paper also explores the impact of a correction threshold to balance under-correction and over-rephrasing, the performance of CoC with extremely long ASR outputs, and the use of pinyin as alternative guidance for error correction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The CoC framework itself is a significant contribution. Moving away from single-pass, full-text replacement or JSON-based error pairs, the segment-by-segment, multi-turn chat approach is novel and addresses key limitations of previous methods, notably stability and fluency. While previous work may have explored multi-turn interaction in other NLP tasks, its application to *full-text ASR error correction* with the specific focus on segment-level guidance is novel.
* **Significance:** The paper presents a clear improvement over existing methods in terms of error reduction. The segment-by-segment approach offers inherent advantages in controlling LLM behavior and maintaining context, crucial for longer documents. The experiments and analysis provide valuable insights into the practical application of LLMs for error correction, particularly in resource-constrained scenarios or when dealing with long ASR outputs. The thorough examination of the correction threshold is particularly valuable for practical deployment.
* **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the shortcomings of existing LLM-based error correction methods.
    *   **Well-Defined Solution:** The CoC framework is well-defined and intuitively explained.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results on the ChFT dataset are compelling and demonstrate the effectiveness of CoC.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The analysis of correction thresholds, long contexts, and pinyin guidance provides valuable insights.
    *   **Use of Open-Source Dataset**: The use of the ChFT dataset increases reproducibility and comparability to future research.
* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Language Scope:** The experiments are primarily focused on Chinese, though some code-switching evaluation is also provided. While the framework itself is language-agnostic, the results might not directly generalize to other languages without further testing.
    *   **LLM Choice:** The use of a specific (internal) LLM makes replication more difficult. While open-source LLMs were used for comparison, the results may vary using different public models.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** While the framework’s various pieces are discussed, explicit ablation studies (e.g., removing the full-text context, removing the segment guidance) to quantify the precise contribution of each component could strengthen the paper.
    *   **Computational Cost:**  The paper does not extensively discuss the computational cost compared to alternative methods. A segment-by-segment multi-turn approach may increase latency, which can be a factor in real-time applications.

* **Potential Impact:** The CoC framework has the potential to significantly improve the accuracy and usability of ASR systems, especially for tasks involving long documents, such as transcription and meeting summarization. The insights into correction thresholds and alternative guidance methods can inform the design of more effective and robust error correction systems. It will likely spur further research into similar segment-by-segment correction methods for various NLP tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates significant novelty in its approach to full-text ASR error correction, providing a well-defined framework (CoC) that addresses crucial shortcomings of prior methods. The comprehensive experimental results on ChFT provide compelling evidence of improved performance. While some limitations exist, such as language scope and lack of thorough ablation studies, the overall contribution is substantial.  The potential impact on ASR accuracy and usability, and the provision of an open dataset, are key indicators of its significance.

### Domain Guidance: A Simple Transfer Approach for a Pre-trained Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01521v1)
- **Authors**: Jincheng Zhong, Xiangcheng Zhang, Jianmin Wang, Mingsheng Long
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in diffusion models have revolutionized generative modeling. However, the impressive and vivid outputs they produce often come at the cost of significant model scaling and increased computational demands. Consequently, building personalized diffusion models based on off-the-shelf models has emerged as an appealing alternative. In this paper, we introduce a novel perspective on conditional generation for transferring a pre-trained model. From this viewpoint, we propose *Domain Guidance*, a straightforward transfer approach that leverages pre-trained knowledge to guide the sampling process toward the target domain. Domain Guidance shares a formulation similar to advanced classifier-free guidance, facilitating better domain alignment and higher-quality generations. We provide both empirical and theoretical analyses of the mechanisms behind Domain Guidance. Our experimental results demonstrate its substantial effectiveness across various transfer benchmarks, achieving over a 19.6% improvement in FID and a 23.4% improvement in FD$_\text{DINOv2}$ compared to standard fine-tuning. Notably, existing fine-tuned models can seamlessly integrate Domain Guidance to leverage these benefits, without additional training.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Domain Guidance" (DoG), a novel transfer learning approach for pre-trained diffusion models.  DoG leverages the concept of classifier-free guidance (CFG) to guide the sampling process towards a target domain. Unlike standard fine-tuning with CFG, which can suffer from catastrophic forgetting and a poorly fitted unconditional guidance model, DoG retains the pre-trained model as an unconditional guiding counterpart. This allows the model to better leverage prior knowledge and improve domain alignment. The authors provide both empirical and theoretical analyses supporting the effectiveness of DoG and demonstrate its superiority over standard fine-tuning and CFG across several transfer learning benchmarks.  They also show that DoG can be easily integrated into existing CFG-fine-tuned models without requiring additional training.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a novel perspective on transferring pre-trained diffusion models.  The key innovation lies in *re-interpreting* the transfer learning problem as conditional generation, explicitly conditioning on the target domain relative to the pre-trained data distribution and formulating a transfer method on top of the existing CFG framework.  The idea of using the original pre-trained model as a fixed unconditional component in the guidance process is a simple yet effective way to address issues with catastrophic forgetting and poor fitting of the unconditional branch during fine-tuning. The observation that standard CFG, when applied with a fine-tuned model, struggles to properly represent the unconditional distribution, leading to OOD issues, is a significant and practical finding.  The theoretical justifications, although building upon existing work, offer insight into why DoG is effective. While not groundbreaking, the idea of leveraging the pretrained diffusion model as a fixed unconditional guiding component for the finetuned model represents a practical and effective innovation.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in generative modeling: adapting large, pre-trained diffusion models to specific domains without incurring excessive computational costs or compromising generation quality.  The results demonstrate substantial improvements in FID and FDDINOV2 scores compared to standard fine-tuning, highlighting the practical benefits of DoG.  The ease of integration into existing fine-tuned models makes it immediately applicable to a wide range of scenarios. The exploration of incorporating the DoG guidance strategy into pretrained large models, such as the stable diffusion models, and the demonstration that DoG can be integrated smoothly with the Low-Rank Adaption (LoRA) parameter-efficient tuning method demonstrates the general applicability and practical significance of DoG. The paper's findings are relevant to both researchers and practitioners working with diffusion models and transfer learning.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear and Well-Written:**  The paper is well-organized and easy to understand, with clear explanations of the method and its rationale.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The extensive experimental evaluation across various benchmarks provides compelling evidence for the effectiveness of DoG.
*   **Theoretical Justification:** The theoretical analysis provides insight into the mechanisms behind DoG's performance.
*   **Practical Applicability:** The method is easy to implement and can be seamlessly integrated into existing workflows.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Theoretical Depth:** While the theoretical analysis provides some insight, it could be further expanded to provide a more complete understanding of DoG's behavior.
*   **Incremental Improvement:** The improvements, while significant, might be considered incremental rather than revolutionary. The core idea builds on established CFG techniques.
*   **Guidance Factor Dependence:** The method still relies on a guidance factor `w` which must be tuned, although the authors report the method is not very sensitive to this hyperparameter.

**Justification for Score:**

Overall, the paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of transfer learning for diffusion models. It is well-written, empirically validated, and theoretically motivated. While the innovation is not revolutionary, DoG provides a simple and effective solution to a practical problem. It's particularly valuable due to its ease of implementation and seamless integration into existing pipelines. The theoretical analysis is a good step, although it could be expanded.

### Redefining technology for indigenous languages
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01522v1)
- **Authors**: Silvia Fernandez-Sabido, Laura Peniche-Sabido
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we offer an overview of indigenous languages, identifying the causes of their devaluation and the need for legislation on language rights. We review the technologies used to revitalize these languages, finding that when they come from outside, they often have the opposite effect to what they seek; however, when developed from within communities, they become powerful instruments of expression. We propose that the inclusion of Indigenous knowledge in large language models (LLMs) will enrich the technological landscape, but must be done in a participatory environment that encourages the exchange of knowledge.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Resignifying Technology for Indigenous Languages" explores the challenges and opportunities of integrating Indigenous languages into the digital world, specifically within the context of Artificial Intelligence (AI) and Large Language Models (LLMs). It offers a broad overview of Indigenous languages globally, highlighting their vulnerability and the causes behind their decline (colonization, globalization, lack of technological representation). The authors examine the impact of various technologies on Indigenous communities, emphasizing the importance of designing technologies that are community-led and participatory. They argue that while AI presents risks, it also holds the potential to revitalize Indigenous languages if Indigenous knowledge systems are integrated into LLMs in a culturally sensitive and collaborative manner. The paper analyzes legal frameworks protecting Indigenous languages and stresses the need for updated legislation that accounts for the intersectional vulnerabilities faced by women, elders, and Indigenous people in the digital age. Finally, the authors propose that involving Indigenous communities in the design of AI tools could result in more equitable and relevant technologies, fostering a deeper understanding and preservation of Indigenous cultures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper provides a valuable overview of a complex and crucial issue – the intersection of Indigenous languages, technology, and AI. The literature review is comprehensive, drawing from a wide range of sources across various disciplines. The paper effectively identifies key factors contributing to the endangerment of Indigenous languages and proposes a nuanced approach to technology integration. It is commendable that the authors emphasize the importance of community-led initiatives and participatory design, acknowledging the potential for technology to both empower and harm Indigenous communities.

However, the paper has some weaknesses. While it highlights the risks associated with AI and LLMs, it could delve deeper into the specific challenges these technologies pose. For example, it doesn't extensively discuss the issue of data bias in LLMs or the potential for cultural appropriation when Indigenous knowledge is incorporated without proper context and consent. Additionally, the proposed solutions (community-led initiatives, participatory design) are somewhat general and lack concrete, actionable steps for implementation. What specific methodologies or frameworks should be used to ensure meaningful participation? How can researchers and developers effectively collaborate with Indigenous communities while respecting their cultural protocols and intellectual property rights? The paper would benefit from a more practical and prescriptive approach.

Another point is that the paper focuses on language revitalization but doesn't adequately explore the economic benefits and opportunities that technological integration might provide. How can technology help create new jobs and income streams within Indigenous communities, while also preserving their cultural heritage?

The **novelty** of the paper is limited. While it synthesizes existing research and perspectives, it doesn't introduce groundbreaking new ideas or empirical findings. However, the **significance** lies in its ability to raise awareness and advocate for a more ethical and inclusive approach to AI development.

**Score:** 6.5

**Justification:**

The score reflects a paper that is informative and well-researched, but which lacks significant novelty and actionable recommendations. The comprehensive overview of the issues surrounding Indigenous languages and technology is a strength. The call for participatory design and community-led initiatives is important. However, the paper falls short in providing concrete strategies and a more in-depth analysis of the specific challenges posed by AI and LLMs. The lack of detailed, practical guidance for implementation diminishes its potential impact on the field. Therefore, while it’s a useful contribution to the conversation, it doesn’t represent a transformative leap. A higher score would have been justified with more specific solutions, empirical validation of those solutions, or a more in-depth critical analysis of AI’s limitations and ethical implications for Indigenous languages.

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 6/10

### Adapting Knowledge Prompt Tuning for Enhanced Automated Program Repair
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01523v1)
- **Authors**: Xuemeng Cai, Lingxiao Jiang
- **Abstract**: Automated Program Repair (APR) aims to enhance software reliability by automatically generating bug-fixing patches. Recent work has improved the state-of-the-art of APR by fine-tuning pre-trained large language models (LLMs), such as CodeT5, for APR. However, the effectiveness of fine-tuning becomes weakened in data scarcity scenarios, and data scarcity can be a common issue in practice, limiting fine-tuning performance. To alleviate this limitation, this paper adapts prompt tuning for enhanced APR and conducts a comprehensive study to evaluate its effectiveness in data scarcity scenarios, using three LLMs of different sizes and six diverse datasets across four programming languages. Prompt tuning rewrites the input to a model by adding extra prompt tokens and tunes both the model and the prompts on a small dataset. These tokens provide task-specific knowledge that can improve the model for APR, which is especially critical in data scarcity scenarios. Moreover, domain knowledge has proven crucial in many code intelligence tasks, but existing studies fail to leverage domain knowledge during the prompt tuning for APR. To close this gap, we introduce knowledge prompt tuning, an approach that adapts prompt tuning with six distinct types of code- or bug-related domain knowledge for APR. Our work, to the best of our knowledge, is the first to adapt and evaluate prompt tuning and the effectiveness of code- or bug-related domain knowledge for APR, particularly under data scarcity settings. Our evaluation results demonstrate that prompt tuning with knowledge generally outperforms fine-tuning under various experimental settings, achieving an average improvement of 87.33% over fine-tuning in data scarcity scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the application of prompt tuning, specifically *knowledge prompt tuning*, to the task of Automated Program Repair (APR). It addresses the challenge of data scarcity in APR, where fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) often underperforms due to limited training examples.  The authors propose adapting prompt tuning, which involves adding task-specific prompts to the input and tuning both the prompts and the model, to enhance APR performance, particularly under data scarcity.  Furthermore, they introduce *knowledge prompt tuning*, which integrates code- or bug-related domain knowledge into the prompt templates.  The study conducts comprehensive experiments using three LLMs of varying sizes and six datasets across four programming languages, comparing the performance of prompt tuning (with and without knowledge) against fine-tuning.  The results show that prompt tuning, especially with the incorporation of domain knowledge, generally outperforms fine-tuning in data scarcity scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Adaptation to APR:** Applying prompt tuning to APR itself is not completely novel, as other works have experimented with prompt tuning to some extent in related code intelligence tasks. However, this paper undertakes a more comprehensive and targeted evaluation *specifically for APR*, and demonstrates a systematic approach to knowledge prompt tuning. It presents clear empirical results demonstrating its effectiveness.
*   **Knowledge Prompt Tuning:** While the idea of incorporating domain knowledge into prompts isn't new in general, the *specific* application to APR with a focus on *code- and bug-related knowledge* and the systematic evaluation of different types of such knowledge represents a clear contribution. They extract and evaluate six different kinds of domain knowledge such as repair action and bug type with corresponding empirical validation.
*   **Comprehensive Empirical Analysis:** The breadth of the empirical analysis, using multiple LLMs, datasets, programming languages, and prompt variations, is a strong point. It provides substantial evidence to support the claims.

**Significance:**

*   **Addressing Data Scarcity:** The work directly tackles a critical practical challenge in APR.  Data scarcity is a prevalent issue, making the findings immediately relevant and applicable. The paper provides a strong alternative to fine-tuning for situations where data is limited.
*   **Practical Guidance:** The detailed experiments and analysis offer valuable insights into the design and selection of prompts, the importance of relevant domain knowledge, and the choice of LLMs for APR tasks. The study provides very solid practical suggestions based on their findings. This helps move the field forward by providing practical guidance.
*   **Potential for Future Research:** The paper opens avenues for future research, such as exploring more advanced prompt tuning techniques, investigating different types of domain knowledge, and adapting the approach to other code intelligence tasks.
*   **Well-Presented Results:** The paper is well-organized, clearly written, and presents results in a readily understandable format.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Model Choice:** The study primarily uses CodeT5+ and GPT-Neo. While these are relevant, exploring more recent and larger models (if computationally feasible) would strengthen the findings. The current limited choices may be partially due to the fact that "OpenPrompt has not been actively maintained for over a year," mentioned in the paper.
*   **Hand-Crafted Templates:** The prompt templates are manually designed, which may be sub-optimal. Automatically generating or optimizing prompts could further improve performance.
*   **Dataset Limitations:**  While the datasets are diverse, some may be less representative of real-world complex bugs. Evaluating on more complex and realistic APR benchmarks would increase the impact.
*   **Lack of Qualitative Analysis:** Although the quantitive metrics are good, a qualitative analysis of the generated patches and how the domain knowledge helps the LLM to find the proper fix is missing.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a strong and comprehensive evaluation of knowledge prompt tuning in Automated Program Repair.  The adaptation of prompt tuning and the incorporation of code- and bug-related domain knowledge contribute to addressing the critical issue of data scarcity in APR. The findings provide valuable practical guidance for researchers and practitioners, and the analysis opens interesting avenues for future work.

The main limitations are that the model choice, prompt template design, and manual integration with domain knowledge need to be improved in future work.
